> **Judge:** Both answers are accurate and cover the same agreements, but B provides richer detail and clearer source citations, giving it higher specificity and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Below are the trade agreements that are mentioned in the material you provided which involve the Republic of Serbia:

| Agreement | Parties | Main purpose / scope (as described in the text) | Where it appears in the source |
|-----------|---------|-----------------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| **Free Trade Agreement between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | Serbia + the four EFTA members (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) | • Creates a free‑trade area for goods and services, liberalises trade in accordance with GATT 1994 Art. XXIV.<br>• Enhances investment opportunities, protects intellectual‑property rights, and gradually opens government‑procurement markets.<br>• Embeds sustainable‑development, environmental, labour‑rights and human‑rights objectives (e.g., references to the Rio, Johannesburg, ILO declarations).<br>• Includes complementary bilateral agreements on agricultural products. | General preamble & objectives [1]‑[3]; trade‑facilitation provisions [4]; reference to bilateral agri‑agreements [5]; procurement cooperation [7]; environmental‑technology promotion [8]. |
| **Free Trade Agreement between the Republic of Albania and Serbia and Montenegro** | Albania + Serbia + Montenegro | The text only gives the title and identifies the three “Contracting Parties.” No further substantive detail is provided in the excerpts. | Mentioned in the heading of the document [6]. |

**What the sources say**

* The EFTA‑Serbia FTA is presented as a comprehensive agreement aimed at “enhancing the competitiveness of their firms in global markets,” “creating favourable conditions for the development and diversification of trade,” and “contributing to the harmonious development and expansion of world trade” [1].  
* Its specific objectives include liberalising trade in goods (GATT 1994 Art. XXIV), boosting investment, protecting IP, opening government procurement, and integrating sustainable‑development goals [2]‑[3].  
* The agreement also calls for simplified trade‑facilitation procedures and cooperation through a Joint Committee [4], and notes that each EFTA state already has a separate bilateral agricultural‑products agreement with Serbia [5].  
* Procurement‑market liberalisation is to be pursued on a “non‑discrimination and reciprocity” basis, with prompt information‑exchange [7].  
* Environmental and green‑economy aspects are highlighted, encouraging “foreign investment… beneficial to the environment” and the development of “green economy, sustainable renewable energy, energy‑efficient and ecolabelled goods and services” [8].

**Limitations**

The excerpts you supplied only mention these two agreements. Serbia may have many other bilateral or multilateral trade accords (e.g., with the EU, CEFTA, WTO, etc.), but those are not included in the provided material. If you need a fuller catalogue of Serbia’s trade agreements, additional sources would be required.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Serbia’s trade‑agreement portfolio (as reflected in the excerpts you provided)**  

| Agreement | Parties involved | Main features mentioned in the text |
|-----------|------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | European Free Trade Association (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) + Serbia | • Establishes a free‑trade area for goods and services, in line with GATT 1994 Art. XXIV. <br>• Aims at liberalising trade in goods, boosting investment, protecting IPR, opening government‑procurement markets, and promoting sustainable development. <br>• Built on market‑economy principles, democratic values and respect for human rights. <br>• References a range of environmental and labour declarations (Stockholm, Rio, Agenda 21, Johannesburg, Rio+20, ILO). <br>• Calls for simplification of trade procedures, cooperation on trade‑facilitation, and information‑exchange on procurement rules. <br>• Explicitly states that the FTA does not exempt the parties from their WTO obligations (Marrakesh Agreement).  <br>• Each EFTA State also has a **separate bilateral agreement on trade in agricultural products** that forms part of the overall free‑trade area.  | [1] – [5] – [7] – [8] |
| **Bilateral agreements on trade in agricultural products** | Serbia + each individual EFTA State (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) | • These agreements are concluded on a bilateral basis and are incorporated into the overall EFTA‑Serbia free‑trade area.  | [5] |
| **Free Trade Agreement between the Republic of Albania and Serbia and Montenegro** | Republic of Albania + Serbia + Montenegro | • The document is only referenced by title; no substantive provisions are quoted in the excerpts. | [6] |

**Summary**

- Serbia is party to a comprehensive **EFTA‑Serbia Free Trade Agreement** that covers goods, services, investment, intellectual‑property, government procurement and sustainable‑development objectives.  
- The FTA is complemented by **separate bilateral agricultural‑product agreements** with each EFTA member.  
- Serbia also participates in a **Free Trade Agreement with Albania (and Montenegro)**, though the provided excerpt does not detail its contents.  

These are the trade agreements involving Serbia that appear in the supplied material.

</details>

### Q2: What is AI governance?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 2.43s | 1.91s (+0.18s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 1 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more facets of AI governance, adds concrete details about human oversight, and cites more sources, outweighing a slight risk of over‑citation.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI governance is the set of structures, policies, and practices that guide how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, built, deployed, and overseen.  In the material you provided it is described as:

* **A shared, multi‑stakeholder responsibility** – it requires coordinated action from governments, industry, academia, civil society and the United Nations system to ensure AI develops in line with societal goals [2].  
* **An organisational model that brings together many actors**, though the current reality may be dominated by a small number of large corporations [3].  
* **A shift from a purely defensive, compliance‑focused activity to a proactive, value‑creating strategic function** that can become a source of competitive advantage for businesses [5].  
* **A practical mechanism for human oversight**, meaning that AI systems are built so that people can intervene according to security standards and personal preferences whenever an autonomous decision is undesirable [8].

Taken together, AI governance is the collective, strategic framework that balances risk mitigation, ethical standards, and innovation by giving diverse stakeholders the authority and tools to shape, monitor, and, when necessary, intervene in AI behaviour.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI governance is the set of policies, processes, and institutional arrangements that guide how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, deployed, and overseen.  In the discussions cited, it is portrayed as:

* **A shared, multi‑stakeholder responsibility** that requires coordinated action among governments, industry, academia, civil society and the United Nations system [2].  
* **A multi‑stakeholder organization**—though in practice it can be dominated by a small number of large corporations [3].  
* **A strategic, value‑creating function** that goes beyond mere risk‑mitigation or compliance, positioning governance as a source of competitive advantage and business success [5].

In short, AI governance is the collaborative framework that sets the rules, standards, and oversight mechanisms for AI, aiming to align technological development with societal values, security standards, and the preferences of users. [2][3][5]

</details>

### Q3: What are india AI policies?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 7.09s | 5.81s (+0.16s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers a broader set of policies and initiatives, provides more concrete details, and cites sources more comprehensively, while both remain accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy landscape is a mix of strategic road‑maps, funding programmes, sector‑wide enablers and emerging governance recommendations.  The key elements that have been publicly announced or documented are:**

| Policy / Initiative | Main Features | Status / Timing | Source |
|---------------------|----------------|----------------|--------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (2018)** | Sets a “strong basis for future regulation” and focuses AI use‑cases in health‑care, agriculture, education, smart cities, infrastructure, mobility and transportation. | Adopted June 2018; still the overarching strategic reference. | [1] |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | Provides a legal framework for personal data handling, which underpins trustworthy AI and data‑driven services. | Enacted 2023. | [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | • Budget > US$1.25 bn for AI infrastructure, innovation, safe & trusted AI.<br>• Funding for computing capacity (free GPUs), skilling, datasets, and sovereign AI research.<br>• Supports creation of “digital data banks, marketplaces and exchanges.” | Announced March 2024; implementation ongoing. | [1], [2], [6], [7] |
| **AI‑Watch / IndiaAI portal (May 2022)** | Public‑facing portal that aggregates AI‑related developments, policy documents, research and industry initiatives. | Launched 2022; continuously updated. | [1] |
| **Task‑Force Recommendations (2023‑24)** | a) Create an inter‑ministerial **National AI Mission** to coordinate activities.<br>b) Set up **digital data banks/marketplaces** for cross‑industry data.<br>c) Develop **operational standards** for AI systems.<br>d) Enact **enabling policies** – data‑ownership rules, sharing rights, tax incentives for AI innovation.<br>e‑f) Draft an **AI education & reskilling strategy** to build skilled talent.<br>g) Participate actively in **international AI governance** discussions.<br>h) Leverage **bilateral partnerships** for social‑economic AI solutions. | Recommendations issued by a government‑appointed task‑force; being incorporated into the 2024 AI Mission. | [6] |
| **Principles for Responsible AI (IndiaAI)** | A set of voluntary principles (e.g., fairness, transparency, accountability) that guide developers and users; currently not codified into binding law. | Published as part of the AI strategy; no enforceable regulation yet. | [1] |
| **Free GPU & Compute Support** | Government‑backed provision of high‑performance GPUs and cloud compute to researchers and start‑ups, aimed at building a sovereign AI ecosystem. | Ongoing under the AI Mission and related “AI Factory” programmes. | [2], [7] |
| **Sector‑specific advisory notes** | Recent advisories on **deepfakes** and **generative AI** to help businesses and the public navigate emerging risks. | Issued 2024. | [1] |
| **Regulatory posture** | India currently **has no binding AI law**; it is pursuing a **principles‑based, flexible framework** that encourages responsible innovation while avoiding overly restrictive or state‑controlled models. | As of the latest writing (2024). | [1] |

### How the pieces fit together

1. **Strategic Vision → Funding & Infrastructure**  
   - The 2018 National Strategy defines priority sectors.  
   - The 2024 AI Mission translates that vision into a concrete budget, free compute resources and data‑exchange platforms.

2. **Governance Foundations**  
   - The Digital Personal Data Protection Act supplies the data‑privacy backbone needed for trustworthy AI.  
   - The “Principles for Responsible AI” and task‑force recommendations outline voluntary standards, operational guidelines and incentives (tax breaks, data‑sharing rules).

3. **Human‑Capital & Ecosystem Development**  
   - AI education, reskilling programmes, and the free‑GPU “AI Factory” aim to create a large, skilled talent pool and a domestic AI supply chain.

4. **International Engagement**  
   - The task‑force explicitly calls for participation in global AI policy dialogues and bilateral partnerships, positioning India as a “middle‑path” between the restrictive European model, the laissez‑faire US approach, and the state‑driven Chinese model.

### Bottom line

India’s AI policy framework is **progressive but still largely non‑legislative**: it combines a **national strategy**, a **multi‑billion‑dollar AI Mission**, **data‑privacy law**, **voluntary responsible‑AI principles**, and **task‑force‑driven recommendations** that together shape a flexible, incentives‑driven environment for AI development, deployment and governance. No binding AI‑specific regulation exists yet, but the government is building the **infrastructure, talent, data ecosystems and standards** that will enable future, more formal regulation if needed.  

**Key citations:** [1] (National Strategy, AI Mission, principles, lack of binding law), [2] (Full‑stack sovereign AI & free GPU support), [6] (Task‑force recommendations), [7] (Progressive policy with free GPUs and funding).

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy landscape – key elements drawn from the supplied sources**

| Policy / Program | Core Features | Source |
|-----------------|---------------|--------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)** | Sets a “strong basis for future regulation” and targets AI use in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart‑cities, infrastructure and smart mobility**. | [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | • Budget of **US $1.25 billion**.<br> • Finances **computing‑infrastructure capacity, skilling, innovation, data‑sets and safe‑trusted AI**.<br> • Issues advisories on **deep‑fakes and generative‑AI**. | [1] |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | Provides a legal framework for personal‑data protection that underpins **trust‑worthy AI** deployments. | [1] |
| **Task‑Force Recommendations (2023‑24)** | a) Create an **inter‑ministerial National AI Mission** to coordinate AI activities.<br>b) Set up **digital data banks, marketplaces and exchanges** for cross‑industry data.<br>c) Develop **operational standards** for AI‑based systems.<br>d) Enact **enabling policies** – data‑ownership/sharing rules, tax incentives for AI innovation.<br>e) Launch an **AI education strategy** and **reskilling programmes**.<br>f) Participate in **international AI‑governance discussions** and bilateral AI partnerships. | [6] |
| **Infrastructure & Resource Support** | The government supplies **free GPUs**, funds AI‑model development, and invests in **energy‑infrastructure** needed for large‑scale compute. | [2], [7] |
| **Public‑Service AI Delivery** | Plans to deliver **free AI‑driven services** (e.g., virtual doctors, tutors, agronomists) through the **Aadhaar‑based digital stack** before encouraging commercial AI applications. | [4] |
| **Regulatory Approach** | No binding AI law yet; India follows a **flexible, principles‑based framework** that encourages responsible innovation while awaiting formal legislation. | [1] |
| **Strategic Positioning** | Leverages India’s existing **digital public‑infrastructure** (UPI, digital identity) to shape a global AI governance stance that avoids overly restrictive European models, laissez‑faire U.S. approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese systems. | [3], [5] |

### Summary

India’s AI policy is a **multi‑pronged, progressive programme** that combines:

1. **Strategic planning** (National Strategy 2018, 2024 AI Mission) with substantial funding for compute, data, and safety.
2. **Legal foundations** (Digital Personal Data Protection Act) to ensure data privacy and trustworthy AI.
3. **Institutional coordination** (inter‑ministerial AI Mission, task‑force recommendations) to align data, standards, incentives, and education.
4. **Infrastructure incentives** (free GPUs, energy support) that lower entry barriers for AI research and development.
5. **Public‑service orientation** (AI services via Aadhaar) to demonstrate social benefits before commercial rollout.
6. **Principles‑based, non‑binding regulation** that encourages innovation while the regulatory framework matures.
7. **International engagement** to shape global AI governance in line with India’s digital‑public‑infrastructure strengths.

These components together constitute the current **AI policy architecture** of India as reflected in the provided context.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**AI diplomacy** is the practice of using diplomatic processes to manage the international dimensions of artificial intelligence.  It covers the full spectrum of activities that states (and other actors) undertake to :

* **Treat AI as a diplomatic topic** – negotiating norms, standards, ethical guidelines, and governance frameworks for AI development and deployment across borders.  
* **Employ AI as a diplomatic tool** – leveraging AI‑driven analytics, forecasting, and communication technologies to enhance reporting, decision‑making, negotiations and strategic communication.  
* **Recognise AI as a factor that reshapes the diplomatic environment** – understanding how AI changes geopolitical and geo‑economic dynamics and therefore influences the context in which traditional diplomacy operates.

In short, AI diplomacy is “how countries manage their international relationships regarding artificial intelligence,” encompassing AI as a subject of diplomatic discussion, a technology that assists diplomatic work, and a force that transforms the geopolitical landscape itself [3][6][7].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**AI diplomacy** is the set of diplomatic activities that nations use to handle artificial intelligence as :

* **A diplomatic topic** – the subject of international discussion, regulation and cooperation (e.g., norms, standards, ethical frameworks).  
* **A diplomatic tool** – the technologies that diplomats employ to improve reporting, analysis, negotiation and strategic communication.  
* **A reshaping force** – the way AI alters the geopolitical and geo‑economic environment in which diplomacy is conducted.

In practice, AI diplomacy covers how countries manage their bilateral and multilateral relationships around AI, coordinate research and development, address risks such as bias or security threats, and leverage AI‑driven tools to make diplomatic work faster and more data‑rich [3]. The concept is framed by a three‑fold distinction that helps policymakers see AI both as a matter to be negotiated and as an instrument that can enhance diplomatic functions [6][7].

</details>

| **Governance & policy turning point** | Multiple briefings (“A tipping point for the Internet: 10 predictions for 2018”, “A Tipping Point for the Internet: Predictions for 2018”, and related briefing papers) all describe **2018 as a tipping point for the Internet and its governance**. ### What the 2018 picture tells us

1. **Scale of connectivity** – By the end of 2018, roughly **4.4 billion** people were expected to have active mobile‑broadband connections, indicating that more than half of the world’s population was online via mobile devices.  
2. **Technical readiness** – IPv6 deployment was advancing, signaling that the underlying Internet architecture was being modernised to support continued growth.  
3. **Economic relevance** – Affordability studies confirmed that cheaper, wider access translates into measurable economic benefits, reinforcing the case for policy measures that lower cost barriers.  
4. **Governance shift** – 2018 was widely framed as a **tipping point** for Internet governance, with emerging issues around AI, gender rights, and cybersecurity prompting calls for updated regulatory and policy frameworks.  
5. **Policy recommendations** – The UN Broadband Commission urged nations to strengthen leadership, training, universal‑service reforms, digital‑skill programs, support for local e‑businesses, legal‑framework adaptation, and tax reductions on ICT equipment to sustain the momentum.

### Bottom line

The collective evidence from the 2018‑focused reports paints a picture of an Internet that was **rapidly expanding in reach (especially via mobile), becoming technically more robust (IPv6), increasingly recognized as an engine of economic growth, and confronting a pivotal moment in governance** that would shape its future regulatory landscape.

</details>

**Serbia is party to two principal sets of international accords that are described in the excerpts you provided:**

| Agreement | Main purpose / scope | Key commitments that appear in the text |
|-----------|---------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Stabilisation and Association Agreement (SAA) with the European Union** | A “political‑economic‑institutional” framework that prepares Serbia for deeper integration with the EU and contributes to regional stability. | • Alignment of Serbian legislation with EU (Community) law in the relevant sectors – e.g., oenological rules, other sectoral regulations【6†L1-L4】. <br>• Commitment to the principles of the UN Charter, OSCE (Helsinki Final Act, Madrid & Vienna conclusions, Paris Charter, Stability Pact)【1†L1-L4】. <br>• Promotion of a free‑market economy and sustainable development, with EU readiness to support Serbia’s economic reforms【1†L5-L7】. <br>• Aim to gradually bring bilateral relations with the EU to the same level as the EU‑Member‑State relations【7†L1-L3】. |
| **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) and Serbia** | Creation of a free‑trade area that deepens trade, investment and regulatory cooperation between Serbia and the four EFTA economies. | • Liberalisation of trade in goods in line with GATT 1994 Art. XXIV【2†L2-L4】. <br>• Expansion of investment opportunities and a progressive opening of government‑procurement markets【2†L5-L8】. <br>• Fair competition, effective protection of intellectual‑property rights【2†L5-L7】. <br>• Integration of sustainable‑development objectives into the trade relationship【2†L9-L11】【5†L1-L3】. <br>• Commitment to uphold democratic principles, human rights and to respect ILO obligations, including ratification of core ILO conventions【3†L1-L3】. <br>• The FTA, together with complementary agricultural agreements, establishes a free‑trade area aimed at “spurring prosperity and sustainable development”【2†L1-L3】. |
| **Other related references** | The documents repeatedly stress that these agreements are part of a broader “instrument” establishing the free‑trade area and that they reaffirm the parties’ dedication to sustainable development, coherence of trade‑environment‑labour policies, and the overall goal of harmonious world‑trade expansion【4†L1-L2】【8†L1-L3】. |

### In short
- **The Stabilisation and Association Agreement** is Serbia’s EU‑oriented pact that obliges Serbia to harmonise its laws with EU legislation, support regional stability, and pursue free‑market and sustainable‑development goals.  
- **The EFTA‑Serbia Free Trade Agreement** creates a free‑trade area, liberalises goods trade, opens investment and procurement markets, safeguards competition and IP, embeds sustainable‑development and labour standards, and rests on shared democratic and human‑rights values.  

These two agreements together constitute the principal “Serbia agreements” referenced in the supplied material.

### Q12: What is Diplos position on AI governance?

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more facets (e.g., geopolitical race, 2019 briefing) with richer detail and broader citation coverage, making it more complete, specific, and well‑referenced.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Diplo (through the Diplo Foundation and its AI Lab) advocates an **inclusive, multistakeholder model of AI governance that is rooted in human‑rights, ethical and philosophical considerations and that balances global coordination with local realities**.

* **Human‑rights and diversity first** – In its IGF 2025 session the Foundation stressed that AI systems must “uphold human rights, reflect diverse perspectives, and be built on balanced data” [2].  
* **Philosophical scrutiny of data‑centric models** – Director Jovan Kurbalija warned against the unquestioned dominance of “data” in governance frameworks, urging a broader philosophical lens on AI policy [1].  
* **Multistakeholder engagement** – Diplo calls for stronger, more relevant multistakeholder fora (e.g., the IGF) and for incentives that draw in a wider range of actors, including the private sector, to ensure active participation [2].  
* **Capacity‑building and diplomatic integration** – The AI Lab’s mandate includes “research and analysis on AI policy, capacity development…analysis of the impact of AI on diplomacy, AI as a tool for diplomatic practice, and AI as a topic for diplomatic negotiations” [3].  
* **Balancing global cooperation with local needs** – The 2025 discussion framed AI governance as “global cooperation meets local needs,” highlighting the need for frameworks that are adaptable to different cultural and regulatory contexts [1].  
* **Ethics and education** – Diplo stresses the intersection of philosophy, technology, education and ethics as central to AI governance, as reflected in Kurbalija’s remarks at IGF 2023 [7].

Taken together, Diplo’s position can be summed up as: **AI governance should be a collaborative, rights‑based process that blends technical expertise with philosophical reflection, ensures balanced data use, involves all relevant stakeholders (states, private sector, civil society, academia), and is supported by ongoing capacity‑building for diplomats and policymakers**. [1][2][3][4][7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Diplo’s stance on artificial‑intelligence governance is that it must be **multistakeholder, rights‑based and grounded in a balance between technology and human wisdom**.  

* **Global cooperation that respects local realities** – At the IGF 2025 “AI governance debated” session, Jovan Kurbalija (Executive Director of the Diplo Foundation) warned against letting “data” become the sole driver of policy and urged that governance frameworks reflect the diverse cultural, social and developmental contexts in which AI is deployed [1].  

* **Human‑rights and diversity as core criteria** – The follow‑up “Diplo/GIP at IGF 2025” discussion stressed that AI systems should **uphold human rights, incorporate diverse perspectives and be built on balanced data**.  It also examined how the private sector and other actors can help ensure these outcomes [2].  

* **Strengthening multistakeholder forums** – Diplo argues that existing platforms such as the Internet Governance Forum need **new incentives and formats to draw broader, more active participation**, because inclusive deliberation is essential for credible AI governance [2].  

* **Capacity‑building and research** – Through its AI Lab, Diplo conducts research, produces policy analyses and runs capacity‑development programmes to help governments and diplomats understand AI’s impact on foreign affairs and to explore AI as both a diplomatic tool and a negotiation topic [3][4].  

* **Philosophical reflection on technology** – Kurbalija repeatedly brings a **philosophical lens** to the debate, questioning the dominance of data‑centric models and reminding policymakers that “human wisdom meets AI power” should guide rule‑making [4][7].  

* **Recognition of the geopolitical race** – Diplo’s 2019 briefing notes that the rapid proliferation of national AI strategies reflects a **“race to become the global leader in artificial intelligence,”** underscoring the need for coordinated, normative governance to avoid a fragmented, competitive landscape [6].  

In sum, Diplo promotes an AI‑governance approach that:

1. **Centers human rights, diversity and balanced data** over pure technical efficiency;  
2. **Integrates local needs with global coordination**;  
3. **Relies on inclusive, multistakeholder processes** (states, private sector, civil society, academia);  
4. **Invests in research, analysis and capacity‑building** to equip diplomats and policymakers; and  
5. **Encourages philosophical and ethical reflection** on the role of AI in society.  

These elements together constitute Diplo’s overarching position on AI governance.   [1][2][3][4][6][7]

</details>

### Q17: digital diplomacy

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 3.21s | 5.64s (+0.09s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.0/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more dimensions (core elements, differences, relevance) with a structured table and richer detail, and it cites sources more systematically, giving it the edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Digital diplomacy** is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives. It encompasses a broad set of practices, such as:

* **Communication and public outreach** – posting statements, speeches, and multimedia content on platforms like Twitter, Facebook, or YouTube to shape a country’s image and reputation [3][5].
* **Virtual negotiations and meetings** – holding diplomatic talks, multilateral conferences, and mediation sessions through video‑conferencing and other online collaboration tools [3][4].
* **Digital platforms for public diplomacy and cultural exchange** – creating websites, apps, or online portals that enable citizen‑to‑citizen interaction, cultural programming, and information sharing [3][7].
* **Agenda‑setting on emerging digital issues** – bringing topics such as privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and internet governance onto the diplomatic agenda [7][4].

Scholars describe digital diplomacy as the **“most comprehensive and widely accepted term to describe the overall transformation of diplomacy in the internet age”** [3]. It is viewed as a **complementary layer to traditional diplomatic tools**, leveraging the networked shifts in demographics and technology to manage change and enhance the reach of diplomatic work [4][7].

In short, digital diplomacy is the integration of internet‑based tools and social media into the core functions of diplomacy—communication, negotiation, mediation, and agenda‑setting—allowing states to operate more efficiently and visibly in the digital era.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Digital diplomacy** – also called *e‑diplomacy* or *web diplomacy* – is the practice of using digital technologies—most notably the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  

### Core elements (from the provided sources)

| Aspect | Description | Source |
|--------|--------------|--------|
| **Technology base** | Relies on the Internet, social‑media channels (Twitter, Instagram, Facebook, etc.), video‑conferencing, and other digital tools. | [3], [7] |
| **Functions** | Enables communication, negotiation, mediation, public‑diplomacy outreach, image‑and‑reputation management, and the creation of digital platforms for cultural exchange. | [3], [4] |
| **Scope** | Extends traditional diplomatic work (bilateral talks, multilateral negotiations) by adding virtual collaborations, rapid information sharing, and networked public engagement. | [4], [8] |
| **Terminology** | “Digital diplomacy” is the most widely accepted umbrella term; “web diplomacy” and “e‑diplomacy” are used interchangeably. | [5], [6] |
| **Agenda impact** | Introduces new topics onto diplomatic agendas—privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and broader internet‑governance issues. | [7], [8] |

### How digital diplomacy differs from traditional diplomacy

1. **Speed & Reach** – Messages can be broadcast instantly to global audiences, bypassing the slower, closed channels of conventional statecraft.  
2. **Public Visibility** – Social‑media use makes diplomatic communication more transparent and subject to public scrutiny, turning diplomats into “digital ambassadors.”  
3. **Multistakeholder Interaction** – Digital platforms enable direct dialogue not only with other states but also with NGOs, tech firms, and individual citizens, expanding the diplomatic “audience.”  
4. **Toolset Expansion** – Virtual meetings, online negotiations, and data‑driven analytics complement (rather than replace) face‑to‑face diplomacy.  

### Why it matters today

- **Geopolitical shifts**: The digital environment reshapes power dynamics, making control over data, platforms, and cyber‑infrastructure a diplomatic priority.  
- **Policy‑making**: Issues like cybersecurity, data sovereignty, and internet governance now sit on foreign‑policy agendas, requiring diplomats to be digitally literate.  
- **Public diplomacy**: Nations use digital channels to shape their image, counter misinformation, and engage diaspora communities.  

In sum, digital diplomacy is the integration of internet‑based tools and social‑media practices into the full spectrum of diplomatic work, transforming how states communicate, negotiate, and set policy in the networked age.  

**Key references:**  
- Definition and breadth of practice: [3]  
- Interdependence with cyber‑diplomacy and agenda‑setting: [4]  
- Emphasis on internet/social‑media as diplomatic tools: [7]  
- Alternate naming and overall impact: [5], [6], [8]

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ITU’s core mandate is to **coordinate and facilitate the global use of telecommunications networks and technologies so that they are interoperable, universally affordable, and widely accessible**.  As the United Nations specialised agency for ICTs, the ITU is tasked with:

* Coordinating the shared global use of the radio‑spectrum and satellite orbits, and establishing worldwide technical standards that enable seamless inter‑connection of communications systems【4】.  
* Promoting and fostering affordable, universal access to telecommunication/ICT networks, services and applications for socially, economically and environmentally sustainable development【1】【2】【3】.  
* Providing technical assistance and voluntary standards to improve communications infrastructure, especially in developing countries【6】.  

In short, the ITU’s mandate is to ensure that networks and technologies are **coordinated, interoperable, and accessible worldwide**.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ITU’s core mandate is to make sure that the world’s communications networks and the technologies that run on them are globally coordinated, interoperable and universally accessible. As the United Nations‑specialised agency for ICTs, the ITU “coordinates the shared global use of the radio spectrum, promotes international cooperation in assigning satellite orbits, improves communications infrastructure in the developing world, and establishes worldwide standards that foster seamless interconnection of a vast range of communications systems” [4]. Its work “facilitates the global interconnection and interoperability of telecommunication facilities, such as devices, networks, and services” [5].  In short, the ITU’s mandate is to ensure that networks and technologies work together worldwide and are affordable and universally available.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

A **multistakeholder platform for dialogue** is an inclusive forum that brings together representatives from government, the private sector, civil society, academia and other interest groups so they can discuss, negotiate and co‑create policies or solutions.  

The sources repeatedly describe such platforms as the **primary mechanism for open, informal networking and structured exchange**:

* They are presented as “the most important … multistakeholder platform” for continuous dialogue among participants【1†L1-L3】.  
* They “facilitate informal networking and dialogue that can lead to better understanding and collaboration” among human‑rights, legal, regulatory and civil‑society actors【2†L1-L4】.  
* In the IGF (Internet Governance Forum) model, the digital dialogues “provide a platform for direct exchange between policymakers, regulators, businesses and civil society”【3†L1-L3】.  
* The same IGF‑style multistakeholder platforms are cited as tools “to facilitate dialogue across different stakeholder groups”【4†L1-L2】.  
* They are explicitly called out as the “best platform for a true example of multi‑stakeholder interaction” when discussing how to foster broader multilateral dialogue【7†L1-L3】.  
* Even in sessions focused on specific issues (e.g., combating illegal content), the need for “the dialogue” is emphasized as a core function of the multistakeholder approach【5†L1-L2】.  

In short, a multistakeholder platform for dialogue is any organized venue—such as the IGF, thematic working groups, or dedicated networking sessions—where diverse stakeholders can meet, share perspectives, and jointly shape policies or initiatives. It serves as a conduit for collaboration among private entities, civil society, and government agencies【8†L1-L2】.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Sorina Teleanu is a senior digital‑policy expert and diplomat who has spent more than 15 years working at the intersection of technology and global governance.  Between 2011 and 2016 she was the alternate representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee, and she has served as an adviser to the Romanian Parliament on ICT‑related legislation and policy [2][4].  

She is currently the **Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation**, where she designs and delivers capacity‑building programmes for policymakers and diplomats, chairs the fellowship selection committee for ICANN, and regularly briefs the diplomatic community on internet and AI issues [4][5][6].  

Teleanu also holds a number of multistakeholder and academic roles: she chaired the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG), was a member of the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum, and serves as a visiting professor at the College of Europe teaching a course on digital diplomacy [2][4].  

In addition to her policy work, she is an author (including a book on the Global Digital Compact), a frequent moderator and speaker at international fora such as the IGF, and an active participant in AI‑related discussions [7][8].  

In short, Sorina Teleanu is a Romanian‑born digital diplomat, researcher, educator and knowledge‑leadership figure who bridges government, multistakeholder bodies and academia on internet governance, AI and broader digital policy issues. [2][5][7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Sorina Teleanu is a senior digital‑policy expert and diplomat who works at the intersection of technology, internet governance and multilateral diplomacy.

* **Current role:** Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation, where she leads research, capacity‑building and policy‑analysis activities on digital diplomacy and internet governance [5][6][8].

* **Professional background:**  
  * 2011‑2016 – Alternate Representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee (GAC) [2].  
  * Former advisor to the Romanian Parliament on ICT‑related legislation and policies [4].  
  * Chair of the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG) [2].  
  * Member of the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum (IGF) [2].  
  * Visiting professor at the College of Europe, teaching a course on digital diplomacy [2].  

* **Capacity‑building and fellowship experience:** Alumna of DiploFoundation’s Internet Governance Capacity‑Building Programme, ICANN’s Fellowship Programme (now a member of its selection committee), the Internet Society’s Next Generation Leadership programme and the European Summer School on Internet Governance [4].

* **Public‑facing work:** Frequently moderates high‑level sessions on AI, internet governance and emerging technologies (e.g., IGF 2023 “Bottom‑up AI and the right to be humanly imperfect”, Policy Network on AI, IGF Parliamentary Track) and is the author of a book on the Global Digital Compact [1][6][7].

In short, Sorina Teleanu is a Romanian‑born digital‑policy specialist, diplomat and educator who currently heads knowledge work at DiploFoundation and has held senior advisory and multistakeholder roles in ICANN, the UN‑IGF, SEEDIG and the Romanian government. [2][4][5][6][7][8]

### Q24: what is digital diplomacy?

| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Answer B covers more dimensions of digital diplomacy, provides clearer structure and examples, and cites sources more comprehensively, while both remain accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy (also called e‑diplomacy or web diplomacy) is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses 

* the **interplay between digital technology and the diplomatic profession** and the resulting shift in the diplomatic environment [2];  
* the **use of the Internet and social media** for communication, negotiation, representation, public‑diplomacy, cultural exchange, and reputation management [3][4][5][7];  
* the **creation of virtual meetings, digital platforms, and online collaborations** that complement traditional diplomatic tools [4][7]; and  
* the **focus on new agenda items** such as privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and broader governance of digital technologies [5][7][8].

In short, digital diplomacy is “the use of the Internet and social media to achieve diplomatic objectives,” turning the networked, ICT‑driven world into a core arena for modern foreign‑policy practice [5][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet and social‑media platforms—to carry out diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses the way diplomacy is reshaped by online tools (e.g., Twitter, Instagram, Facebook), virtual meetings, and digital platforms for public outreach, as well as the new policy issues that arise in cyberspace such as privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity [2][3][4][5][7][8].

</details>

> **Judge:** Both answers adequately define ping‑pong diplomacy and its historical context, are accurate, and cite sources, with only minor differences in citation detail.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the practice of using sport – specifically the exchange of table‑tennis (ping‑pong) players – as a tool for building diplomatic bridges and fostering peace between rival states.  

In the early 1970s the United States and the People’s Republic of China arranged a series of friendly matches and player exchanges.  The U.S. team’s visit to China (and the reciprocal visits that followed) was portrayed as a symbolic gesture of goodwill that helped break down ideological barriers, opened the door to high‑level diplomatic talks, and ultimately led to the normalisation of U.S.–China relations【2†L1-L5】【4†L1-L5】.  

The episode is widely cited as a landmark example of “sports diplomacy,” showing how a seemingly minor, informal sporting interaction can have a profound impact on international understanding and peace【1†L1-L4】【3†L1-L3】.  

Thus, ping‑pong diplomacy refers to the early‑1970s U.S.–China table‑tennis exchanges that served as a catalyst for renewed diplomatic engagement and cultural exchange between the two nations.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the informal, sport‑based diplomatic initiative that began in the early 1970s when American and Chinese table‑tennis teams were exchanged for a series of friendly matches.  The encounters were seen as a symbolic gesture of goodwill that broke down ideological barriers, opened channels for cultural exchange, and paved the way for high‑level talks that eventually normalised relations between the United States and the People’s Republic of China [2][4].  It is widely cited as a classic example of **sports diplomacy** – the use of sport to promote international understanding, peace, and the rebuilding of strained bilateral ties [1][3].

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based ways to address the WTO’s current challenges**

| WTO issue (as described in the excerpts) | Trade‑agreement‑based solution mentioned in the context | How the solution works |
|---|---|---|
| **Barriers to cross‑border commerce and lack of predictability** | *Multilateral trade agreements* (e.g., the Marrakesh Agreement, GATT, GATS, ITA/ITA II, TBT, TRIPS) | By codifying common rules and standards, these agreements lower tariff and non‑tariff obstacles, create a “level playing field,” and give businesses a clear, transparent framework for operating internationally【5】. |
| **Need for a forum to negotiate and update rules** | *WTO’s own negotiating forum* (Ministerial Conferences) | The WTO convenes Ministerial Conferences (e.g., 2017, Tenth Ministerial) that serve as the venue for members to negotiate new or revised agreements that reduce trade obstacles and promote growth【3】【4】. |
| **Unfair trading practices – IP theft, forced technology transfer, subsidies, state‑owned‑enterprise distortions, overcapacity** | *Working group on WTO reform* (EU‑U.S. initiative) | Leaders have pledged a dedicated working group to reform the WTO, targeting the specific unfair practices listed above【6】. |
| **Digital‑economy‑specific challenges (data flows, trust, security)** | *Digital trade agreements* that embed consistent IP and data‑exchange rules (built on TRIPS and related standards) | Such agreements “reduce barriers for businesses operating across borders and foster trust in the digital economy by providing secure and reliable frameworks for data exchange”【1】【2】. |
| **Implementation gaps for the Trade Facilitation Agreement (TFA)** | *Regional trade agreements* that pilot “climate‑smart” or other sector‑specific facilitation measures | Regional pacts can tailor WTO‑level obligations to local conditions, helping members meet TFA commitments more effectively【7】. |
| **Dispute‑settlement uncertainties** | *Strengthening the WTO Dispute Settlement System* (recognised as a “cornerstone” of the multilateral system) | The system itself is highlighted as a tool to enhance security and predictability, and reforms to it are implied as part of broader agreement‑driven solutions【8】. |

### Summary
The context points to a suite of **trade‑agreement solutions** for WTO‑related problems:

1. **Adopt or update multilateral agreements** (GATT, GATS, ITA, TBT, TRIPS) to create uniform, predictable rules.  
2. **Use the WTO’s Ministerial Conference process** to negotiate new rules that directly target emerging barriers.  
3. **Launch targeted reform working groups** (e.g., the EU‑U.S. initiative) to tackle unfair practices such as IP theft and forced technology transfer.  
4. **Negotiate digital‑trade accords** that embed secure data‑exchange standards and harmonised IP protections, thereby building trust in the digital economy.  
5. **Leverage regional agreements** to pilot and implement specific facilitation measures (e.g., climate‑smart trade) that complement WTO obligations.  
6. **Reinforce the WTO dispute‑settlement mechanism** to ensure that any rule‑based solution remains enforceable and credible.

Together, these agreement‑based approaches aim to reduce obstacles, increase transparency, and restore confidence in the multilateral trading system.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based solutions that the WTO system itself points to for tackling its own shortcomings**

| Issue / Challenge | Agreement‑based remedy (as described in the WTO‑related texts) | Source |
|-------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|
| **Fragmented rules and lack of predictability** | Adopt and expand multilateral agreements that set **consistent rules and standards** (e.g., the Marrakesh Agreement, GATT, GATS, ITA/ITA II, TBT, TRIPS). These create a transparent, predictable framework for all members. | [5] |
| **Barriers to cross‑border digital commerce** | Negotiate **digital‑trade provisions** (e.g., data‑flow rules, secure data‑exchange frameworks) within existing agreements or new digital‑trade pacts. Such provisions lower technical and regulatory obstacles and build trust in the digital economy. | [1], [2] |
| **Unfair trading practices (IP theft, forced technology transfer, subsidies, overcapacity)** | Set up a **dedicated WTO reform working group** to craft or update agreements that specifically address these practices, including stronger IP‑protection clauses and rules on state‑owned enterprises. | [6] |
| **Insufficient dispute‑resolution confidence** | Strengthen the **WTO Dispute Settlement System** as a “cornerstone” that guarantees security and predictability, ensuring that any rule‑breach is promptly and fairly adjudicated. | [8] |
| **Implementation gaps at the regional level** | Leverage **regional trade agreements** to pilot and operationalise WTO‑wide measures (e.g., Trade Facilitation Agreement) that are tailored to local challenges, thereby smoothing the path for global adoption. | [7] |
| **Overall obstacles to trade and uneven playing field** | Use the WTO **Ministerial Conferences** as forums to negotiate new or revised agreements that explicitly aim to **reduce obstacles** and **ensure a level playing field** for all members, fostering economic growth and development. | [3], [4] |

### In short
The WTO’s own documents suggest that **the solution lies in expanding, harmonising, and updating trade agreements**—both multilateral (GATT, TRIPS, etc.) and sector‑specific (digital‑trade, regional facilitation pacts)—while reinforcing the dispute‑settlement mechanism and creating focused reform groups to tackle unfair practices. These agreement‑based tools collectively address the structural issues that the WTO faces today.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI politics can be summed up as a state‑driven, “strategic‑sovereignty” agenda that treats artificial intelligence as a core pillar of national power.**  

| Dimension | What the policy says | How it is being pursued | Why it matters |
|-----------|---------------------|------------------------|----------------|
| **Economic productivity** | AI is “pivotal for India’s economic productivity” and is expected to lift output across sectors. | The government is funding AI research, providing **free GPUs**, and encouraging the creation of **large‑scale models** (e.g., BharatGPT).  Energy‑infrastructure upgrades are also being planned to support compute‑intensive workloads. | A home‑grown AI ecosystem is meant to generate new jobs, boost exports of AI‑enabled services, and keep the country competitive in the global digital economy. |
| **Military & security** | AI is framed as essential for “military power” and “information control.” | The same public‑infrastructure backbone (Aadhaar, UPI) is being leveraged to develop AI‑driven defence‑analytics, surveillance, and decision‑support tools. | Embedding AI in defence gives India a technological edge and reduces reliance on foreign vendors for critical security systems. |
| **Information control & public services** | The strategy calls for **free AI services** (tele‑medicine, tutoring, agronomy) delivered through the **Aadhaar stack** before any “disruptive business applications” are rolled out. | Public‑sector portals will host AI‑powered chat‑bots and recommendation engines that are directly linked to citizens’ unique IDs, ensuring the state can shape the flow of information. | This creates a “sovereign data‑pipeline” that can be used for both social welfare and for monitoring public discourse. |
| **Governance model** | India wants a **middle‑path** in AI governance: not as restrictive as the EU, not as laissez‑faire as the US, and not a state‑controlled model like China. | The country is drawing on its **digital public‑infrastructure experience** (UPI, digital identity) to design a governance framework that is **risk‑based** and **sector‑specific**.  The National AI Strategy proposes **domain‑specific ethical councils** to draft privacy, ethics, and security guidelines for each sector. | This positioning allows India to claim leadership in “responsible AI” while preserving flexibility for domestic innovators. |
| **Sovereign AI stack** | The “Full‑Stack Sovereign AI” vision (CoRover BharatGPT) calls for an end‑to‑end ecosystem—from data collection (Aadhaar, UPI) to compute (government‑provided GPUs) to model deployment—under Indian ownership. | Funding streams, hardware subsidies, and dedicated research institutes are being set up to keep the stack under national control. | A sovereign stack reduces dependence on foreign cloud providers and safeguards strategic data. |

### Core Themes of India’s AI Politics

1. **AI as a national security and economic asset** – The state treats AI the same way it treats nuclear or space capabilities: a strategic lever for growth, defence, and geopolitical influence.  
2. **Integration with existing digital identity and payments infrastructure** – Aadhaar and UPI are the “data highways” that will feed AI services, ensuring that the benefits (free doctors, tutors, agronomists) are delivered at scale and that the state retains visibility over the data flow.  
3. **Balanced regulatory stance** – By avoiding the extremes of the EU, US, or China, India aims to craft a **risk‑based, sector‑specific regulatory regime** anchored by ethical councils, thereby attracting private investment while maintaining sovereign oversight.  
4. **State‑led provision of core AI resources** – Free GPUs, funding for large‑model training, and dedicated energy infrastructure signal a willingness to subsidize the foundational layers of AI development.  
5. **Information governance** – Early deployment of free, state‑run AI services creates a “soft‑power” channel for shaping public opinion and controlling misinformation, complementing the more overt security uses.  

### In short

*India’s AI politics is a coordinated, state‑centric strategy that couples **economic ambition**, **defence modernization**, and **information governance** with a **sovereign digital infrastructure** (Aadhaar, UPI) and a **moderate, risk‑based regulatory framework**. The goal is to make AI a public good while retaining strategic control over data, models, and the narratives they enable.*  

**Sources:**  
- AI’s role in economic, military and information domains and the push for free citizen services via Aadhaar [1][2][4][8]  
- Positioning India between EU, US, and China models, leveraging UPI and digital identity [3][6]  
- Domain‑specific ethical councils in the National AI Strategy [5]  
- Government provision of GPUs, funding, and energy needs for a sovereign AI stack [7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s AI politics is framed as a **strategic, state‑driven programme that ties artificial‑intelligence development to three core national interests – economic productivity, defence (military power) and information control**.  The policy narrative repeatedly stresses that AI must first be used to deliver **free, public‑service‑oriented applications** (e.g., virtual doctors, tutors and agronomists) through the existing Aadhaar‑based digital identity infrastructure, before the country encourages “disruptive” commercial AI products [1][2][4].

Key pillars of the Indian AI political agenda include:

| Pillar | Description | Source |
|--------|-------------|--------|
| **Leverage existing digital public infrastructure** | India will build on the success of UPI, Aadhaar and other sovereign platforms to create a “full‑stack” AI ecosystem, providing free GPUs, funding for model development and dedicated energy resources. | [7] |
| **Balanced global positioning** | The government seeks a middle path in AI governance – avoiding the **over‑regulatory European model**, the **laissez‑faire American approach**, and the **state‑centric Chinese system** – while promoting its own standards rooted in its digital‑identity experience. | [3][6] |
| **Domain‑specific ethical oversight** | The National AI Strategy calls for **sector‑specific ethical councils** that will draft guidelines on privacy, ethics and security for each application domain (finance, health, agriculture, etc.). | [5] |
| **National security & information control** | AI is portrayed as essential for **military capability** and for **controlling information flows**, reinforcing the state’s strategic autonomy. | [1][4][8] |
| **Economic productivity & inclusive growth** | By delivering free AI‑powered services to citizens, the policy aims to boost **productivity across agriculture, education and health**, thereby driving broader economic growth. | [1][2] |

In sum, **India’s AI politics** is a coordinated effort to harness AI as a tool of national power—economically, militarily and informationally—while grounding that effort in sovereign digital infrastructure, moderate regulatory philosophy, and sector‑specific ethical governance.

</details>

| Pillar | What the WAIGF says it entails | How to put it into practice (from the excerpts) |
|--------|--------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| **Inclusivity** | “A digital future that is inclusive, human‑centric and resilient” [2][4] | • Ensure participation of **diverse stakeholders** – governments, private sector, civil society, and especially youth [3][5]  <br>• Build **capacity and expertise** among these groups so they can engage effectively [7] |
| **Safety & Rights‑Respect** | “Safe and inclusive digital future is a shared responsibility” [5] | • Coordinate **cross‑sectoral efforts** to protect users (e.g., data protection, online safety) [3][5] <br>• Align local and national initiatives with rights‑respecting standards [6] |

| **Equity & Sustainability** | “Inclusive, equitable and sustainable” [2] | • Design services that reach **underserved communities** (rural, gender‑minority, persons with disabilities) <br>• Embed **environmental sustainability** in digital projects (energy‑efficient data centers, e‑waste management) |
| **Collaboration** | “Collaboration between government, private sector, and citizens” [3] | • Create **multi‑stakeholder platforms** (e.g., high‑level sessions, open forums) to co‑design policies [5][8] <br>• Share best practices and resources across **regional and national** levels [6] |
| **Human‑Centric Design** | “Human‑centric” [2][4] | • Prioritize **user needs and rights** in technology design <br>• Conduct **participatory assessments** to ensure solutions are culturally appropriate and accessible |

### Practical Steps (derived from the excerpts)

1. **Launch Multi‑Stakeholder Forums** – replicate the “High Level Youth IGF” and “Open Forum #12” formats to bring together government, industry, academia, and civil society for dialogue and joint planning [3][5][6][7][8].

2. **Capacity‑Building Programs** – develop training, mentorship, and knowledge‑exchange initiatives that empower a broad range of actors to take part in digital‑policy discussions [7].

3. **Policy Coordination Mechanisms** – establish inter‑agency committees or regional task forces that align safety, privacy, and resilience standards across jurisdictions [3][5].

4. **Inclusive Service Design** – apply universal design principles and conduct impact assessments to guarantee that new digital services are accessible to all demographic groups [2][4].

5. **Resilience Planning** – integrate risk‑assessment tools, incident‑response protocols, and redundancy measures into national digital strategies [1][2].

6. **Rights‑Respecting Frameworks** – adopt and enforce legislation that safeguards freedom of expression, data protection, and non‑discrimination online [6].

7. **Sustainable Infrastructure Investment** – prioritize low‑carbon technologies, renewable‑energy‑powered data centres, and responsible e‑waste recycling [2].

By following these pillars and steps—each echoed throughout the WAIGF opening, keynote, and wrap‑up remarks—you can move toward a **digital future that is inclusive, safe, resilient, equitable, and sustainable** for West Africa and beyond.  

*Sources: [1]–[8]*

</details>

**Key Take‑aways for Building an Inclusive and Resilient Digital Future**

| Pillar | What the WAIGF 2023 discussions stress | Practical steps you can take |
|--------|----------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Inclusivity** | The digital future must be *human‑centric, equitable and rights‑respecting* for all citizens, especially marginalized groups【2†L1-L2】【4†L1-L2】. | • Conduct stakeholder mapping that deliberately includes women, youth, persons with disabilities and rural communities.<br>• Co‑design policies and services with these groups to ensure relevance and accessibility.<br>• Adopt open‑data standards and multilingual interfaces to lower barriers to participation. |
| **Resilience** | A resilient digital ecosystem can withstand shocks (e.g., cyber‑attacks, natural disasters) and continue to deliver essential services【1†L1-L2】【5†L1-L2】. | • Invest in redundant infrastructure (e.g., multi‑carrier internet back‑bones, cloud‑based backups).<br>• Develop national and regional incident‑response frameworks that involve public‑private partners.<br>• Regularly test continuity plans through simulations and tabletop exercises. |
| **Safety & Security** | Safety is a shared responsibility that requires coordinated cross‑sector action【3†L1-L3】【5†L1-L2】. | • Implement robust data‑protection regulations aligned with international human‑rights standards.<br>• Promote security‑by‑design in all digital products and services.<br>• Run public awareness campaigns on safe online behaviours. |
| **Collaboration Across Sectors** | Government, private sector, civil society and citizens must work together to achieve the vision【3†L1-L3】【5†L1-L2】. | • Set up multi‑stakeholder platforms (e.g., high‑level IGF sessions, open forums) for continuous dialogue.<br>• Create joint funding mechanisms (public‑private‑civil‑society trusts) for capacity‑building projects. |
| **Capacity Building** | Building expertise among diverse stakeholders enables effective participation【7†L1-L2】. | • Offer training programs on digital literacy, cybersecurity, and policy‑making for NGOs, SMEs, and community groups.<br>• Establish mentorship networks linking tech firms with local innovators.<br>• Provide toolkits and open‑source resources to lower entry costs. |
| **Local & National Initiatives** | Concrete examples of inclusive, rights‑respecting digital futures are emerging at local and national levels【6†L1-L2】. | • Document and scale successful pilots (e.g., community broadband, e‑health portals).<br>• Align national digital strategies with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and regional frameworks such as the West African Digital Agenda. |

### A concise roadmap

1. **Define a shared vision** – Adopt a statement like “a digital future that is inclusive, human‑centric, resilient and safe” (the recurring WAIGF mantra)【2†L1-L2】【4†L1-L2】.  
2. **Map stakeholders & responsibilities** – Identify government agencies, private‑sector players, civil‑society groups, and citizen representatives; assign clear roles for policy, infrastructure, security, and capacity‑building.  
3. **Create a multi‑sector governance body** – A high‑level forum (e.g., a national IGF) that meets regularly to coordinate actions, monitor progress, and resolve conflicts.  
4. **Launch capacity‑building programmes** – Target diverse groups with digital‑literacy, cybersecurity, and policy‑engagement training【7†L1-L2】.  
5. **Implement resilient infrastructure** – Build redundant networks, adopt cloud‑based backup, and develop incident‑response protocols.  
6. **Enact safety & rights‑based regulations** – Ensure data protection, privacy, and online safety laws reflect human‑rights standards.  
7. **Pilot and scale local initiatives** – Test inclusive solutions (e.g., community Wi‑Fi, accessible e‑services) and replicate successful models nationally【6†L1-L2】.  
8. **Monitor, evaluate, and adapt** – Use measurable indicators (access rates, incident response times, stakeholder satisfaction) to refine policies continuously.

### Bottom line
The WAIGF consensus is clear: **building an inclusive and resilient digital future is a collective, cross‑sector effort that hinges on human‑centred design, robust security, and ongoing capacity building**【1†L1-L2】【3†L1-L3】【5†L1-L2】【7†L1-L2】. By institutionalising multi‑stakeholder collaboration, investing in resilient infrastructure, and empowering all citizens to participate, West Africa—and any region—can move toward a digital ecosystem that is safe, equitable, and future‑proof.

</details>

India’s AI roadmap is being shaped around a **“sovereign‑first”** model that ties the country’s existing digital public‑infrastructure (Aadhaar, UPI, digital identity) to a new, home‑grown AI ecosystem.  The main elements that repeatedly appear in the sources are:

| Pillar / focus | What it means for the strategy | Source |

| **Compute & cloud sovereignty** | Invest in domestic, high‑performance compute clusters (“sovereign compute”) that can run AI models locally and securely. | [5], [7] |
| **Services sovereignty** | Deploy AI‑driven public services (e‑health, tutoring, agronomy advice) through the Aadhaar identity stack, offering them for free to citizens before opening the market to commercial‑grade applications. | [1], [3] |
| **Model‑size focus** | Concentrate on “highly performant, low‑cost” models in the 30‑200 billion‑parameter range rather than chasing trillion‑parameter AGI systems, keeping costs and energy use manageable. | [6] |
| **Leverage digital public infrastructure** | Use the success of UPI, Aadhaar and other government‑run platforms as a springboard for AI governance and data‑sharing, positioning India as a global AI policy voice. | [2] |
| **Balanced regulatory stance** | Craft agile, multistakeholder regulations that avoid the heavy‑handedness of the EU model, the laissez‑faire of the US, and the state‑centric approach of China. | [2] |
| **Data‑access for research** | Streamline access to high‑quality, curated datasets to fuel domestic AI research and innovation. | [8] |
| **Human‑capital & collaboration** | Upskill the workforce and foster public‑private‑academic partnerships to sustain AI innovation. | [5] |

**Key take‑aways**

1. **Three‑pillar architecture** – energy, compute/cloud, and services sovereignty form the backbone of the plan ([5]; [7]).
2. **Public‑service first** – free AI‑enabled services (doctor, tutor, agronomist) delivered through Aadhaar are the initial rollout, with commercial applications to follow ([1]; [3]).
3. **Mid‑size model emphasis** – India will prioritize cost‑effective, high‑performing models (30‑200 B parameters) rather than competing in the trillion‑parameter “AGI race” ([6]).
4. **Governance balance** – The strategy seeks a middle path in AI regulation, leveraging India’s digital ID and payments infrastructure while staying clear of the extremes seen in Europe, the US, or China ([2]).
5. **Data and talent** – Easy access to quality datasets and a focus on skilling/collaboration are seen as essential enablers ([5]; [8]).

Together, these elements describe a uniquely Indian AI strategy that couples **sovereign infrastructure** with **wide‑scale public service delivery**, while positioning the country as a pragmatic player in global AI governance.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s AI roadmap is being shaped around a **“sovereign‑first”** model that ties together infrastructure, talent, services and governance.

| Pillar | What it means | Key points from the sources |
|--------|----------------|------------------------------|
| **Energy & Compute Sovereignty** | Build home‑grown, low‑cost, high‑performance compute capacity and secure the power needed to run it. | “India’s AI strategy is built on three pillars: energy sovereignty, compute and cloud sovereignty, and services sovereignty” [7]; “invest in sovereign compute infrastructure and energy” [5]. |
| **Services Sovereignty** | Use the nation’s digital public‑infrastructure (Aadhaar, UPI, digital identity) as the backbone for AI‑driven public services. | “Provide free AI services (doctors, tutors, agronomists) to citizens through the Aadhaar stack” [1][3]; “leveraging its successful digital public infrastructure experience (UPI, digital identity)” [2]. |

| **Governance & Data** | Adopt agile, multistakeholder regulations that give researchers streamlined access to quality data while avoiding the extremes of overly restrictive EU rules, laissez‑faire US approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese models. | “Position itself globally in AI governance…while avoiding pitfalls of overly restrictive European models, laissez‑faire American approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese systems” [2]; “streamlined access to good quality data sets for research and innovation” [8]. |

**Overall thrust**

- **Economic productivity & military power**: AI is seen as a driver of growth and strategic capability [1][3].  
- **Free public AI services first**: Before commercial AI products roll out, the government aims to embed AI in citizen‑facing services (health, education, agriculture) via Aadhaar.  
- **Competitive positioning**: By focusing on sovereign compute, low‑cost models and a robust data ecosystem, India seeks a middle path—leveraging its digital infrastructure strengths without mimicking the restrictive or uncontrolled models of other major powers [2].  

In short, India’s AI strategy is a three‑pillar framework—energy/computing sovereignty, services sovereignty built on its digital ID ecosystem, and an innovation‑driven talent/governance agenda—designed to deliver affordable AI to the masses while positioning the country as a responsible global AI leader.

### Core Objectives (as defined at launch)

1. **Neutral, inclusive debate space** for digital actors and policymakers.  
2. **Strengthening participation** of small and developing countries in Geneva‑based digital policy processes.  
3. **Supporting Geneva‑based IG and ICT institutions** (e.g., UN‑OG, ITU, WTO).  
4. **Monitoring and analysing digital policies** to inform evidence‑based governance.  
5. **Bridging policy silos** across different sectors and disciplines.  
6. **Providing tools and methods** for both physical and virtual engagement that can be adopted elsewhere [5][7].

### Operational Home

- **Operator:** DiploFoundation (a Swiss‑based nonprofit that builds capacity for diplomacy and digital governance).  
- **Funding/Support:** Swiss government (through the Federal Office of Communications) and multilateral partners [5][6].

### Significance

The launch of GIP marked a deliberate effort to **connect the fragmented landscape of Internet governance**—bringing together multilateral bodies, civil society, academia, and the private sector under a single, **multidisciplinary platform**. By foregrounding the voices of **small and developing nations**, the platform seeks to counterbalance the dominance of larger states and commercial actors in global digital policy debates.

### Q43: How does AI affect international negotiations?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.00s | 5.71s (+0.11s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 3 |
| **Winner** |  |  |

> **Judge:** Both answers comprehensively cover the question, stay accurate to the likely sources, give concrete examples, and cite sources effectively, making them equally strong.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI is reshaping international negotiations in several concrete ways:

1. **Managing complexity and scale** – Multilateral talks often involve dozens of parties and thousands of pages of text. AI tools can “track positions of multiple countries and stakeholders” and “process vast amounts of documents,” allowing negotiators to see where each side stands in real‑time and to spot emerging patterns that would be invisible to the human eye [6][8].

2. **Synthesising massive public input** – In the UNESCO negotiation of the “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence,” more than 55 000 public comments were received. AI was used to “integrate them,” turning an overwhelming volume of feedback into actionable insights for the drafting process [1].

3. **Augmenting human judgment** – Diplomats stress that negotiation remains a fundamentally human activity, but AI can “augment humans” by providing data‑driven support—identifying historical precedents, suggesting counter‑arguments, and highlighting where consensus is possible [1][7][8].

4. **Facilitating co‑creation and collaboration** – By visualising where countries are positioned (e.g., via a Zoom‑style map of positions), AI helps diplomats discover unexpected alignments (such as Russia and the UK sharing a stance) and enables rapid outreach to build coalitions [1].

5. **Putting new topics on the agenda and shaping geopolitics** – The broader impact of AI on diplomacy includes “putting new topics on the international agenda” and “challenging geostrategic relations,” which in turn changes the substance and dynamics of negotiations [2][4][5].

**In sum**, AI affects international negotiations by turning massive, fragmented information into a coherent, searchable, and visualised knowledge base; by highlighting where parties converge or diverge; and by acting as a decision‑support tool that amplifies the capacity of human diplomats to negotiate more efficiently, inclusively, and strategically [1][6][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI is reshaping the way states negotiate by acting as a **force‑multiplier for diplomats** rather than replacing the human element.  The material you provided highlights several concrete ways this happens:

1. **Managing multilateral complexity** – In large‑scale talks (e.g., the UNESCO “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence” that involved 193 countries), AI was used to map where each delegation stood, making it easier to spot alignments (e.g., Russia and the UK) and to steer the discussion toward common ground [1].  Similar observations note that AI can “track positions of multiple countries and stakeholders” and “process vast amounts of documents, and identify historical precedents and counter‑arguments” [6][8].

2. **Integrating massive public input** – After the draft recommendation was released, UNESCO received **55 000 public comments**.  AI tools were employed to synthesize that flood of feedback, turning an otherwise unmanageable data set into actionable insights for the negotiators [1].

3. **Augmenting the human negotiation process** – Speakers repeatedly stress that AI should be used to **augment humans** in what is fundamentally a human activity.  By providing real‑time analytics, visualising where parties converge or diverge, and surfacing relevant evidence, AI helps diplomats focus on the substantive political work rather than on data‑crunching [3][7].

4. **Shaping the diplomatic agenda** – Beyond the mechanics of a single negotiation, AI is expected to **put new topics on the international agenda**, challenge existing geostrategic relations, and create both opportunities and concerns (e.g., human‑rights implications).  This broader impact means that AI becomes a subject of negotiation itself, influencing the content and framing of future treaties [2][4][5].

**In summary**, AI affects international negotiations by:

- **Tracking and visualising each party’s positions** in real time, reducing uncertainty.  
- **Processing and summarising huge document sets** (including public comments), turning raw data into usable knowledge.  
- **Identifying precedents and counter‑arguments**, giving negotiators a richer evidentiary base.  
- **Facilitating co‑creation and consensus‑building**, as diplomats can see where unexpected alliances form.  
- **Altering the diplomatic agenda**, because the technology itself becomes a policy issue that states must negotiate.

All of these functions serve to **augment human diplomats**, making multilateral talks more efficient, transparent, and data‑informed while preserving the fundamentally human nature of negotiation [1][6][8][2].

</details>
