<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy is a mix of strategic planning, funding programmes, sector‑focused initiatives and a nascent regulatory framework.**  

| Element | What it does | Key details from the context |
|---------|--------------|------------------------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence** | Sets the overall direction for AI development and future regulation. | Launched in June 2018; targets AI use in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart‑cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transportation**【1†L1-L4】. |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | Provides the bulk of the financial muscle for building a sovereign AI ecosystem. | **$1.25 billion** allocated in March 2024 for **computing‑infrastructure capacity, skilling, innovation, datasets, and safe & trusted AI**【1†L5-L7】. |
| **IndiaAI portal** | Centralised information hub for AI‑related projects, research and policy. | Launched by the government in **May 2022**【1†L9-L10】. |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | First comprehensive data‑privacy law that also shapes how AI can process personal data. | Mentioned as part of the regulatory landscape【1†L11-L12】. |
| **Advisories on deepfakes & generative AI** | Non‑binding guidance to mitigate emerging risks. | Issued alongside the 2024 mission as “advisories addressing deepfakes and generative AI”【1†L7-L8】. |
| **Regulatory stance** | Currently **principles‑based, not yet binding**. | Israel is cited as a parallel example; the text notes that **no AI regulations in India currently carry the force of law**【1†L13-L14】. |
| **Strategic positioning** | Guides how India wants to be seen in the global AI governance arena. | Emphasises leveraging existing **digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, digital identity)** while avoiding the extremes of **over‑restrictive European models, laissez‑faire US approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese systems**【3†L1-L4】【5†L1-L4】. |
| **Policy goals** | Blend economic productivity, national security and inclusive service delivery. | Calls for free AI services (e.g., doctors, tutors, agronomists) built on the **Aadhaar stack** before commercial roll‑outs【4†L1-L4】. |

### Summary  

India’s AI policy is built around a **strategic, sector‑focused roadmap (the 2018 National Strategy)**, now backed by a **large‑scale funding mission (the 2024 India AI Mission)** that invests in infrastructure, talent, datasets and trustworthy AI. The government has created a **dedicated portal (IndiaAI)** for transparency and coordination, and it is beginning to issue **risk‑based advisories** (e.g., on deepfakes). While a **comprehensive data‑protection law (2023) provides a regulatory baseline**, there are **no binding AI‑specific statutes yet**; the approach remains **principles‑based** and flexible.  

Strategically, India aims to **leverage its proven digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar) to become a global AI leader**, positioning itself between the restrictive European model, the laissez‑faire US model, and the state‑driven Chinese model, and to deliver **free, citizen‑centric AI services** before commercialising disruptive applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy is a multi‑layered, evolving framework that combines a long‑term strategic vision with concrete funding programmes and sector‑specific safeguards.**  

| Component | What it does | Key points from the context |
|-----------|--------------|-----------------------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence** (June 2018) | Sets the overall direction for AI development in the country. | Focuses on AI applications in **healthcare, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport**. It is intended to lay the groundwork for future regulation. [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | A large‑scale implementation programme backed by a dedicated budget. | **$1.25 billion** allocated for: <br>• Building **computing‑infrastructure capacity** (e.g., free GPUs, cloud resources). <br>• **Skilling** the workforce and fostering AI talent. <br>• Supporting **innovation** (funding AI models, research). <br>• Creating and curating **datasets**. <br>• Ensuring **safe and trusted AI** (risk‑based guidelines, deep‑fake and generative‑AI advisories). [1] |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | Provides a legal baseline for data privacy, which underpins trustworthy AI. | Treats personal data as a protected asset, requiring consent, purpose limitation, and security safeguards—critical for AI systems that process large volumes of personal information. [1] |
| **Principles‑based, non‑binding regulatory approach** | India has not yet enacted AI‑specific statutes that carry the force of law. | The government is advancing **flexible, principles‑based guidelines** to encourage responsible innovation while avoiding overly prescriptive regimes. [1] |
| **Sector‑specific advisory notes** | Targeted guidance for emerging AI risks. | Recent advisories address **deep‑fakes** and **generative AI**, signalling a move toward risk‑based oversight without full‑scale legislation. [1] |
| **Strategic positioning in global AI governance** | Leverages existing digital public infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar) to shape a balanced AI ecosystem. | The policy aims to avoid the extremes of **over‑restrictive European models**, **laissez‑faire U.S. approaches**, or **state‑controlled Chinese systems**, instead building on India’s proven digital platforms to foster trustworthy AI. [3][5] |
| **Public‑service AI delivery** | Uses AI to provide free, high‑impact services to citizens. | Plans to embed AI‑driven **doctors, tutors, agronomists** into the Aadhaar stack before rolling out commercial AI applications. [4] |

### Summary of India’s AI policy

1. **Strategic Vision** – The 2018 National Strategy outlines priority sectors and the intent to create a “strong basis for future regulation.”
2. **Heavy Investment** – The 2024 India AI Mission earmarks **$1.25 bn** for infrastructure, talent, innovation, data assets, and safety mechanisms.
3. **Data‑Centric Trust Framework** – The 2023 Digital Personal Data Protection Act supplies the privacy backbone needed for responsible AI.
4. **Principles‑Based Governance** – Rather than binding AI statutes, India is issuing advisory guidelines (e.g., on deep‑fakes, generative AI) and adopting a flexible, risk‑based approach.
5. **Leverage Existing Digital Infrastructure** – AI policy is tightly linked to platforms like **UPI** and **Aadhaar**, aiming to deliver free AI services to citizens while positioning India as a balanced player in the global AI governance arena.

Thus, India’s AI policy can be described as a **strategic, investment‑driven, principles‑based framework** that emphasizes sectoral impact, data protection, risk‑aware guidance, and the use of its robust digital public infrastructure to foster trustworthy AI development. [1][3][4]

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Key fisheries‑related clauses that repeatedly appear in the FTAs and that are most likely to be raised in WTO‑level talks for Yaonde**

| Area of the provision | What the text says (examples) | Why it matters for WTO negotiations |
|----------------------|--------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Preservation of existing fisheries relationships** | “The trade aspects of this Agreement should not affect the functioning of the Fisheries Agreement… the volume of the mutual fisheries possibilities … should continue to be maintained at a satisfactory level” [1] | Signals that a trade pact must respect pre‑existing fisheries‑management regimes – a point the WTO will examine when assessing whether a new agreement undermines or duplicates existing multilateral obligations. |
| **Commitments on sustainable‑development and subsidies** | “Fulfil the objectives of the 2030 Agenda … prohibiting certain forms of fisheries subsidies … eliminating subsidies that contribute to IUU fishing … refrain from introducing new such subsidies” [2]; reference to UNCLOS, UN Fish Stocks Agreement, FAO Compliance and Port‑State Measures [2] | Directly relevant to the WTO’s ongoing “fisheries‑subsidies” negotiations (the “Fisheries Subsidies Agreement”). The WTO will look for concrete bans on capacity‑enhancing subsidies and alignment with UN/FAO instruments. |
| **Recognition of the sector’s socio‑economic importance** | “The Parties recognize the importance of the fisheries sector for their development and for the livelihood of their fishing communities, including artisanal fisheries” [3]; “recognise the importance of conservation and sustainable management … for present and future generations” [3] | Provides a policy justification for special and differential treatment (SDT) or for allowing preferential measures that support small‑scale and artisanal fishers – a common WTO discussion point. |
| **Eligibility / nationality rules for vessels** | Detailed criteria on registration, flag, ownership, crew nationality, and a “Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products” [4]; note that the rule does **not** apply to fish taken from the other Party’s EEZ [4] | Such “origin‑type” rules are often scrutinised under the WTO’s “rules of origin” and “non‑discrimination” principles; the Working Group clause shows a mechanism for ongoing dialogue, which the WTO may encourage. |
| **Co‑operation on diversification, competitiveness and rural development** | “Diversification, adjustment, and improvement of the competitiveness of the agricultural, aquaculture, forestry, and fishing subsectors” [5]; “cooperate in the field of fisheries and aquaculture” [6] | Highlights that trade agreements can contain development‑oriented fisheries chapters – WTO members may argue these are permissible under Article II:2(b) (development‑related exceptions). |
| **Preferential market‑access / rules‑of‑origin for fish products** | “Fish and fish products from ACP countries that comply with EU’s rules of origin … imported … free of tariffs” [7] | Shows how fisheries can be linked to tariff‑free treatment – a classic WTO “preferential‑treatment” issue that must meet the “generalised system of preferences” (GSP) criteria. |
| **WTO‑level agenda on trade‑distorting practices** | “WTO members would indicate the provisions, including disciplines on the trade‑distorting practices (including fisheries subsidies) they seek to clarify and improve” [8] | Confirms that fisheries subsidies are on the WTO’s negotiation table; any bilateral FTA clause that touches subsidies will be cross‑checked against the emerging WTO agreement. |

### Bottom line for Yaonde’s WTO talks
- **Subsidy discipline** – any FTA clause that bans or limits capacity‑enhancing subsidies, aligns with UNCLOS/FAO instruments, and commits to the 2030 SDG targets will be viewed favorably and can serve as a model for the multilateral fisheries‑subsidies text.  
- **Sustainable‑management commitments** – references to conservation, traceability, and IUU‑fishing measures demonstrate compliance with existing international fisheries law, which the WTO will expect.  
- **Preferential‑access rules** – detailed rules‑of‑origin and vessel‑eligibility criteria must be consistent with WTO non‑discrimination and GSP rules.  
- **Development‑oriented language** – acknowledging artisanal fisheries and rural development can justify special treatment under WTO provisions for developing economies.  

These are the fisheries provisions that are most likely to be cited, compared, or negotiated in the WTO context for Yaonde.

</details>

**Key fisheries‑related clauses that have repeatedly shown up in the FTAs you supplied – and that are therefore the “building blocks” WTO negotiators in Yaonde are likely to look at – can be grouped into four broad categories.**

|----------|------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| **1.  Preservation of the existing fisheries relationship** | “the trade aspects of this Agreement should not affect the functioning of the Fisheries Agreement … the volume of the mutual fisheries possibilities … should continue to be maintained at a satisfactory level” [1] | WTO members often want to be sure that a new trade pact does not undermine pre‑existing fisheries‑management regimes (e.g., bilateral fisheries agreements, regional fisheries organisations). |
| **2.  Sustainable‑development and subsidy discipline** | • “fulfil the objectives of the 2030 Agenda … prohibiting certain forms of fisheries subsidies … eliminating subsidies that contribute to IUU fishing … refraining from introducing new such subsidies” [2]  <br>• “recognise the importance of ensuring the conservation and sustainable management of living marine resources” [2]  <br>• “the Parties recognise the importance of conservation and sustainable management of fisheries” [3] | These clauses give WTO negotiators a template for **subsidy‑discipline** (the “blue‑box” rules that the WTO is currently discussing) and for linking trade commitments to the 2030 Sustainable Development Goals. |
| **3.  Alignment with international fisheries law and technical standards** | • References to UNCLOS, the UN Fish‑Stocks Agreement, the FAO Compliance Agreement and the FAO Port‑State Measures Agreement [2]  <br>• Promotion of “FAO’s Voluntary Guidelines for Catch Documentation Schemes … traceability schemes” [2] | WTO members want any fisheries chapter to be **consistent with multilateral law** (UNCLOS, FAO instruments) and to incorporate **traceability / catch‑documentation** standards that can be monitored under the WTO’s transparency obligations. |
| **4.  Operational and market‑access measures** | • Detailed vessel‑eligibility rules (registration, flag, ownership, crew nationality) and a “Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products” [4]  <br>• “Cooperate in the field of fisheries and aquaculture” [6]  <br>• “Preferential market access … fish and fish products … imported tariff‑free” [7]  <br>• “Diversification, adjustment and improvement of competitiveness of the fishing subsector” [5] | These provisions give WTO negotiators concrete **rules of origin**, **eligibility criteria**, and **co‑operation mechanisms** that can be built into a WTO‑compatible fisheries chapter (e.g., a “green‑box” for sustainable products, or a special‑and‑differential treatment clause for developing‑country fisheries). |
| **5.  Future‑oriented clarification of subsidy disciplines** | “WTO members would indicate the provisions … they seek to clarify and improve … including fisheries subsidies” [8] | Directly signals that WTO talks will likely include a **dedicated agenda item on fisheries‑subsidy disciplines**, and the above clauses can be used as reference points for what language is already accepted in regional FTAs. |

### How these provisions can be used in the Yaonde WTO negotiations  

1. **Baseline guarantee** – Adopt language similar to [1] to assure parties that the new WTO agreement will not erode existing bilateral or regional fisheries management arrangements.  
2. **Subsidy‑discipline template** – Use the prohibitions and “no‑new‑subsidy” pledge from [2] as a model for the “blue‑box” rules that the WTO is currently negotiating on fisheries subsidies.  
3. **Legal consistency** – Cite the explicit references to UNCLOS, the UN Fish‑Stocks Agreement and FAO conventions in [2] to make the WTO chapter **compatible with existing multilateral fisheries law**.  
4. **Technical standards** – Incorporate the traceability and catch‑documentation requirements from [2] (FAO Voluntary Guidelines) as a **minimum standard** for any preferential market‑access concession.  
5. **Eligibility & cooperation mechanisms** – Borrow the vessel‑criteria and Working Group set‑up from [4] and the cooperation language from [6] to create a **joint fisheries‑product committee** under the WTO framework.  
6. **Preferential market access** – Mirror the tariff‑free clause for compliant fish products in [7] to design a **rules‑of‑origin** carve‑out for sustainable fish and fishery products.  
7. **Development focus** – Echo the “diversification and competitiveness” language of [5] and the recognition of artisanal fisheries in [3] to justify **special‑and‑differential treatment** for developing‑country fisheries sectors.  

**In short, the fisheries provisions that are most relevant for WTO negotiations in Yaonde are those that (i) protect existing fisheries relationships, (ii) embed sustainable‑development and subsidy‑discipline commitments, (iii) align with UNCLOS/FAO instruments and traceability standards, and (iv) set out concrete eligibility, cooperation and market‑access rules that can be translated into WTO‑compatible language.**

| **Digital public‑goods strategy** | Norway’s broader digital development strategy emphasised open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic principles, a stance that shaped its pandemic‑era digital policies. | 【8】 |

**In short:** Norway responded to COVID‑19 by rapidly bolstering economic protections, encouraging remote work, limiting disruptive restructuring, accelerating digitalisation across the economy, and enforcing strict privacy rules that even led to the shutdown of its contact‑tracing app. All of these measures were framed within a longer‑term vision of green growth, competitiveness and open digital public goods.

**AI assistants created by the Diplo Foundation**

The Diplo Foundation has built a family of AI‑driven reporting assistants that combine custom diplomatic data‑sets, specialised algorithms and large‑language‑model (LLM) capabilities.  The most‑often‑mentioned members of this family are:

* **DiploGPT** – a dedicated generative‑AI tool that produces “just‑in‑time” transcripts, summaries and key‑question extractions from high‑level diplomatic meetings (e.g., the UN Security Council) [2], [8].  
* **The AI‑reporting / human‑AI hybrid system** – a broader reporting platform that can ingest live statements, run text‑analysis pipelines and output structured reports for a range of UN fora (Security Council, Internet Governance Forum, etc.) [1], [5], [8].  

Both assistants are described as “AI assistants” that the Foundation’s analysts use to turn raw debate data into usable diplomatic intelligence.

---

**Which assistant was used to track UN General Assembly speeches?**

The tool that was specifically deployed to monitor and analyse the speeches delivered at the UN General Assembly (the 78th session) was the **AI‑reporting system tested during the UNGA 78 and the Internet Governance Forum 2023**.  This system provided live transcripts and event‑level summaries of the Assembly’s statements [1].

So, while DiploGPT is the flagship assistant for Security‑Council reporting, the **AI‑reporting system (the human‑AI hybrid reporting tool)** was the one that tracked the UN General Assembly meetings’ speeches.  

**Sources**  
- The AI reporting system tested at the UNGA 78 for transcripts and summaries [1].  
- Description of the AI‑reporting / hybrid system that generates just‑in‑time reports [5], [8].  
- Mention of DiploGPT as the AI tool used for UN Security‑Council reporting [2].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The Diplo Foundation has built a **human‑AI hybrid reporting assistant** that it calls **DiploGPT** – a custom AI‑reporting tool that combines specialised diplomatic data‑sets, custom algorithms and a large‑language‑model to generate real‑time transcripts, summaries and key‑question extracts from multilateral meetings [2][5][8].

The same assistant was **deployed to follow the speeches delivered at the United Nations General Assembly**.  In the “Policy Meets Tech – Journey Diary” the system was tested during the UN General Assembly 78 (and again at the Internet Governance Forum 2023) to produce transcripts and event‑summaries of the plenary debates [1].  Therefore, the AI assistant used to track UN GA speeches is DiploGPT (the Diplo Foundation’s AI‑reporting tool).

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**How to Build Trust‑Centred AI – Key Take‑aways from the Provided Sources**

| Dimension | What the source says | Practical implication |
|-----------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| **Transparency & Understandability** | “Building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust.” (Refs [1], [7]) | Design AI models and interfaces that expose their decision logic, data provenance, and performance metrics in plain language for both developers and end‑users. |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… every element of the architecture needs to have trust built in.” (Ref [3]) | Foster long‑term partnerships among developers, operators, regulators, and users. Formalize trust through service‑level agreements, joint governance boards, and continuous communication. |
| **Trust Embedded Across the Stack** | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature.” (Ref [6]) | • **Infrastructure** – Secure hardware, tamper‑evident logs, and provenance‑aware data pipelines.<br>• **Platform** – Enforce robust identity‑management, audit trails, and model‑version control.<br>• **Application** – Provide explainable outputs, user‑centric controls, and recourse mechanisms. |
| **Stakeholder Involvement** | “How to build trust in AI systems and the role of different stakeholders in this process.” (Ref [5]) | Involve regulators, civil‑society groups, and affected communities early in design, testing, and deployment to co‑define trust criteria and accountability structures. |
| **Rights‑Respecting Design** | “Building trustworthy and rights‑respecting technologies.” (Ref [4]) | Align AI behavior with human rights standards (privacy, non‑discrimination, freedom of expression). Conduct impact assessments and embed mitigation measures directly into the system. |
| **Continuous Trust Maintenance** | “We need to work very closely to make sure the trust is not broken.” (Ref [3]) | Implement ongoing monitoring, transparent incident reporting, and rapid remediation processes to prevent erosion of trust over time. |

### A Concise Blueprint for “Trust‑First” AI

1. **Make the system transparent from day 1** – publish model cards, data sheets, and real‑time dashboards that explain how decisions are made.  
2. **Embed trust at every architectural layer** – treat security, provenance, and explainability as core requirements, not optional add‑ons.  
3. **Cultivate relational trust** – establish formal governance bodies that include all relevant stakeholders and maintain open communication channels.  
4. **Align with rights‑based principles** – conduct human‑rights impact assessments and embed safeguards (e.g., bias mitigation, privacy‑by‑design).  
5. **Implement a trust‑maintenance loop** – continuous monitoring, transparent reporting of anomalies, and swift corrective actions keep confidence high.  

By weaving transparency, relational governance, layered safeguards, stakeholder participation, and rights‑respecting practices into the DNA of AI projects, organizations can move from “trust as an afterthought” to “trust as a foundational pillar.”  

**Sources:** [1], [3], [4], [5], [6], [7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**How to Build Trust‑Centred AI (as reflected in the provided excerpts)**  

| Key Principle | What the sources say | How to apply it |
|---------------|----------------------|-----------------|
| **Make systems transparent and understandable** | “So building transparent systems that are understandable, I think that's one way to build trust.” – [1] & [7] | Publish model documentation, data‑lineage charts, and decision‑logic explanations that can be inspected by developers, regulators, and end‑users. |
| **Embed trust into the architecture from the start** | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature.” – [6] | Design trust‑related controls (audit logs, access‑control, provenance tracking) at the infrastructure level, propagate them through platform services, and expose them in the application UI. |
| **Treat trust as a relational, ongoing process** | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… we need to work very closely to make sure the trust is not broken.” – [3] | Establish long‑term partnerships between AI developers, operators, and users; set up regular check‑ins, joint governance bodies, and clear escalation paths for incidents. |
| **Involve all relevant stakeholders** | “…how to build trust in AI systems and the role of different stakeholders in this process.” – [5] | Create multi‑stakeholder forums (industry, academia, civil society, regulators) to co‑design trust criteria, review deployments, and share best practices. |
| **Make trust a core business value** | “Firstly, it's building trust.” – [4] | Align corporate KPIs and performance metrics with trust outcomes (e.g., compliance rates, user satisfaction, incident reduction). |
| **Avoid treating trust as an after‑thought add‑on** | “Trust… needs to be built foundationally… rather than as an add‑on feature.” – [6] | Integrate trust checks into the development lifecycle (design → test → deploy) rather than adding a “trust layer” only after the product is live. |
| **Maintain consistency to prevent trust erosion** | “…make sure the trust is not broken.” – [3] | Implement continuous monitoring, transparent reporting of system changes, and rapid remediation when deviations occur. |

### Practical Steps Summarized
1. **Design for Transparency** – publish model cards, data provenance, and explainability dashboards.  
2. **Architect Trust‑First** – embed provenance, audit, and security controls at the infrastructure level and carry them through platforms to applications.  
3. **Cultivate Relationships** – set up joint governance committees with all parties that will build, operate, and use the AI.  
4. **Engage Stakeholders Early** – involve regulators, civil‑society groups, and end‑users in defining trust criteria.  
5. **Tie Trust to Business Metrics** – make trust a measurable KPI (e.g., trust‑score, compliance ratio).  
6. **Continuous Oversight** – monitor performance, publish regular trust‑reports, and address breaches promptly.  

By following these principles—transparent design, foundational integration, relational stewardship, multi‑stakeholder participation, and ongoing monitoring—organizations can **build AI systems that are trusted by the people who create them and the people who use them**.

</details>

### Q54: How does ECMAScript differ from JavaScript?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.61s | 4.62s (+0.07s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more facets (origin, role, scope, yearly editions, engine examples) with concrete details and consistent citations, making it more complete and specific while remaining accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**ECMAScript is the *standard* that defines the language; JavaScript is one of the *implementations* that follows that standard.**

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| What it is | A language **specification** maintained by Ecma International (ECMA‑262 and related specs). | A concrete **programming language** created by Netscape (and later by other vendors) that implements the ECMAScript spec. |
| Origin | Developed from several earlier technologies, most notably **JavaScript (Netscape)** and **JScript (Microsoft)**, and formalised as a standard starting in 1996【1†L1-L4】. | The original name given to the Netscape implementation of the language; the term “JavaScript” is used for the actual runtime that browsers expose to developers. |
| Role | Describes the syntax, semantics, built‑in objects (maps, sets, binary arrays, Unicode handling, etc.) and the yearly‑released editions (e.g., ECMAScript 2016)【1†L5-L9】. | Provides the actual engine (V8, SpiderMonkey, Chakra, etc.) that executes code written according to the ECMAScript specification. |
| Scope of use | Defined as a **vendor‑neutral, cross‑platform** language; increasingly used beyond browsers, e.g., in embedded systems and micro‑controllers【3†L1-L4】. | Historically tied to web browsers, but also shipped in many runtimes (Node.js, Deno) that claim “JavaScript” compatibility. |
| Relationship | Implementations such as JavaScript must conform to the ECMAScript spec; they may add extra features but must remain compatible with the standard. | Implements the ECMAScript spec; when a browser says it supports “ECMAScript 2022”, it means its JavaScript engine conforms to that edition. |

In short, **ECMAScript is the formal, open‑development specification; JavaScript is the name of the language as delivered by browsers and other runtimes that implement that specification**【1†L1-L4】【5†L1-L3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **formal, vendor‑neutral specification** that defines how the language must behave. It is the standard maintained by Ecma International (ECMA‑262 and related specs) and describes the core syntax, built‑in objects, data types (e.g., maps, sets, binary numeric arrays) and Unicode handling that all compliant implementations must provide【1†L1-L7】【1†L8-L12】.  

JavaScript, by contrast, is the **original implementation** of that specification created by Netscape (and later by Microsoft as JScript). It was one of the “originating technologies” that gave rise to the ECMAScript standard【1†L1-L3】. In everyday use the term “JavaScript” is often used to refer to the language as it is implemented in browsers, but technically it is a particular product that **conforms to** the ECMAScript spec.

So the key differences are:

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| Nature | Standard/specification (ECMA‑262, etc.) | Concrete implementation (Netscape’s JavaScript, Microsoft’s JScript, browser engines) |
| Scope | Defines the language’s syntax, built‑ins, Unicode support, etc. for any platform | Provides the actual runtime that executes code according to the spec |
| Governance | Developed openly by Ecma TC39, updated yearly | Developed by individual vendors (e.g., Netscape, Microsoft, V8, SpiderMonkey) who may add non‑standard extensions |

Thus, ECMAScript is the “rules book,” while JavaScript is a widely‑used “player” that follows those rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**How to Build Trusted AI – Key Take‑aways from the Provided Sources**

| Pillar / Action | What it Means | Evidence from the Context |
|-----------------|---------------|---------------------------|
| **Transparency & Explainability** | Make AI systems understandable and open about how they work. | “building transparent systems that are understandable… ” [1]; “trust building requires transparency, explainability…” [5] |
| **Traceability (Data & Model Lineage)** | Keep a clear record of the data, models, and processes used, so every component can be audited. | “we need to trace the systems, the models, the data that we use for AI.” [2]; “the ability to trace systems and data” [6] |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | Ensure people can intervene, review, and override AI decisions when needed. | “human‑in‑the‑loop governance” [5] |
| **Clear Policies & Ownership** | Define who is responsible for each part of the AI value chain and set explicit rules for permissible experimentation. | “clear ownership along the value chain” [6]; “clear policies defining allowed AI experimentation areas” [5] |
| **Understanding Limits & Failure Modes** | Know where the model may break, its performance boundaries, and how it behaves under stress. | “knowing the limits and failure points of models” [6] |
| **Benchmarking & Performance Evaluation** | Regularly test AI against standards and real‑world scenarios to verify reliability. | “benchmarking performance” [6] |
| **Security & Robustness** | Protect AI assets from tampering and ensure resilience against attacks. | “ensuring security” [6] |
| **Embedding Trust Across the Stack** | Integrate trust considerations at every layer (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an after‑thought. | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers… rather than as an add‑on feature” [3] |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | Build long‑term collaborative relationships among stakeholders; maintain trust through ongoing communication and evaluation. | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… spending some time on transparency and evaluation… make sure the trust is not broken.” [4] |
| **Leverage Existing International Institutions** | Use established global bodies to set ethical foundations and governance, avoiding the need for brand‑new structures. | “utilizing existing international institutions rather than creating entirely new governance structures.” [7][8] |

### Practical Steps to Implement These Pillars

1. **Document Data & Model Lineage** – Create registries that log data sources, preprocessing steps, model versions, and training parameters.  
2. **Publish Model Cards & System Cards** – Summarize capabilities, intended use, limitations, and known failure points for each AI artifact.  
3. **Integrate Human Review Points** – Design workflows where critical decisions trigger human validation before final action.  
4. **Define Governance Policies** – Draft clear rules on permissible AI experiments, risk thresholds, and escalation procedures.  
5. **Run Regular Benchmarks & Audits** – Compare model outputs against industry standards and conduct third‑party security assessments.  
6. **Establish Ownership Maps** – Assign responsibility for data, model development, deployment, and maintenance across the value chain.  
7. **Foster Stakeholder Collaboration** – Hold periodic transparency meetings with partners, regulators, and users to maintain relational trust.  
8. **Align with International Frameworks** – Adopt guidelines from bodies such as OECD, ISO, or the EU AI Act to ensure global consistency.

By weaving these elements into the design, development, and operation of AI systems, organizations can create **trusted AI** that is transparent, accountable, secure, and aligned with societal expectations.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**How to Build Trusted AI – Key Take‑aways from the Sources**

| Pillar / Action | What it Means | Evidence |
|-----------------|---------------|----------|
| **Transparency & Explainability** | Make AI systems and their decisions open and understandable to users and stakeholders. | “Building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust” [1]; “Trust building requires transparency, explainability…” [5] |
| **Traceability (Trace‑ability of models, data & value‑chain ownership)** | Keep a clear record of where data comes from, how models are trained, and who owns each component of the AI pipeline. | “We need to trace the systems, the models, the data that we use for AI” [2]; “The ability to trace systems and data… clear ownership along the value chain” [6] |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | Ensure that people can intervene, validate, or override AI outputs, especially in high‑risk contexts. | “Human‑in‑the‑loop governance” [5] |
| **Clear Policies & Defined Experimentation Boundaries** | Publish rules that spell out what AI can and cannot be used for, reducing uncertainty for partners and the public. | “Clear policies defining allowed AI experimentation areas” [5] |
| **Performance Benchmarking & Knowledge of Limits** | Regularly test AI against standards, document failure modes, and communicate the system’s reliability limits. | “Knowing the limits and failure points of models, benchmarking performance” [6] |
| **Security & Robustness** | Protect AI assets from tampering, adversarial attacks, and data breaches. | “Ensuring security” [6] |
| **Embedding Trust Across the Stack** | Design trust into every layer (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than adding it later as an after‑thought. | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers… rather than as an add‑on feature” [3] |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust & Ongoing Collaboration** | Build long‑term partnerships, keep communication open, and act quickly to repair any breach of trust. | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… spend time on transparency and evaluation… make sure the trust is not broken” [4] |
| **Leverage Existing International Institutions** | Use established bodies (e.g., ISO, OECD, UNESCO) to coordinate standards rather than inventing brand‑new governance structures. | “Utilizing existing international institutions rather than creating entirely new governance structures” [7][8] |

### Practical Steps for an Organization

1. **Document the AI lifecycle** – data provenance, model versioning, and ownership records (traceability).  
2. **Publish model cards or datasheets** that explain purpose, performance metrics, known limitations, and security safeguards.  
3. **Integrate human‑in‑the‑loop checkpoints** at critical decision points (e.g., medical diagnosis, credit scoring).  
4. **Adopt a “trust‑by‑design” architecture**: embed security, audit logs, and explainability modules from the hardware layer up to the user‑facing application.  
5. **Create a governance framework** with clear policies on permissible AI use, regular audits, and a rapid‑response plan for incidents.  
6. **Benchmark regularly** against industry standards and publish results to demonstrate reliability.  
7. **Engage stakeholders continuously** – customers, regulators, and partners – to maintain relational trust and address concerns promptly.  
8. **Align with international standards** (e.g., ISO/IEC 42001 for AI risk management) to benefit from shared best practices and avoid duplication of effort.

### Bottom Line
Building trusted AI is not a single checkbox; it requires a **holistic, layered approach** that combines technical safeguards (traceability, security, benchmarking) with **human‑centric governance** (transparency, explainability, policies, relationship building) and **leverages existing global institutions** for standardisation. Implementing these pillars together creates a resilient trust foundation that can scale with AI deployments.

</details>

### Q59: internet standardization

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.09s | 6.66s (+0.09s expansion) |
| Score avg | 3.8/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 2 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer B covers a broader range of relevant aspects, provides more concrete details and citations, and uses the sources more systematically than Answer A.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Internet standardization – what the provided sources tell us**

1. **Purpose and impact**  
   - Standardisation is the process of creating common technical specifications (e.g., TCP/IP, DNS, SSL, HTML, XML) that all devices, networks and services can follow.  
   - By doing so, it makes the Internet **interoperable** – an iPhone, an Android phone, a laptop or any other endpoint can communicate reliably with one another [1][3][5].

2. **Key benefits highlighted in the IGF 2023 material**  
   - **Seamless operation** – The success of the global Internet in “functioning seamlessly across devices and networks” is directly attributed to standardisation [1][3].  
   - **Safety and security** – Standards help guarantee that critical services meet internationally recognised levels of **performance and safety**, reducing vulnerabilities and enabling a more secure Internet [6].  
   - **Sustainability** – A common set of standards also supports an **eco‑friendly, inclusive policy** for the Internet, helping mitigate environmental risks and promote sustainable growth [2].  
   - **Policy and governance** – Standardisation provides a “level playing field” for infrastructure providers and embeds widely‑accepted good practices, shaping the behaviour of the “reasonable man” in the digital realm [6][7].

3. **Who creates the standards?**  
   - International bodies such as the **International Organization for Standardization (ISO)**, the **International Telecommunication Union (ITU)** and, most prominently for Internet protocols, the **Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)** develop and maintain these technical documents [4][6].

4. **Examples of core Internet standards**  
   - **TCP/IP** – the foundational transport and addressing protocol suite.  
   - **Domain Name System (DNS)** – translates human‑readable names to IP addresses.  
   - **Secure Sockets Layer (SSL)/TLS** – provides encrypted communication.  
   - **HTML / XML** – define how content is structured and exchanged [8][5].

---

### In short
Internet standardisation is the globally coordinated effort to define, publish and adopt technical specifications that ensure devices and networks can work together safely, efficiently and sustainably. It is driven by bodies like the IETF, ISO and ITU, and its outcomes underpin everything from basic connectivity (TCP/IP) to security (SSL/TLS) and content formatting (HTML, XML). The IGF 2023 discussions stress that without such standards the Internet could not deliver the seamless, secure and environmentally responsible service that users worldwide now expect [1][2][6][7].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Internet standardization – a concise overview**

| Aspect | What the sources say | Why it matters |
|--------|----------------------|----------------|
| **Core purpose** | “Standardisation has been instrumental in allowing the internet to function seamlessly across devices and networks” and “ensuring interoperability”【1†L1-L4】【3†L1-L2】 | Guarantees that an iPhone, an Android phone, a laptop or any other device can exchange data without custom‑made adapters. |
| **Key organizations** | Standards are set by bodies such as the **International Organization for Standardization (ISO)**, the **International Telecommunication Union (ITU)** and the **Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)**【4†L1-L3】 | These groups bring together engineers, operators, researchers and policy‑makers to produce globally‑accepted technical specifications (e.g., TCP/IP, DNS, SSL). |
| **Security & safety** | International standardisation “provides a level playing field … and assures that the important services meet internationally recognized levels of performance and safety”【6†L1-L4】 | By codifying best‑practice security mechanisms (e.g., TLS/SSL), standards reduce vulnerabilities and make the whole ecosystem more trustworthy. |
| **Sustainability & inclusivity** | Standardisation is “vital in mitigating the risks associated with the internet and promoting a sustainable approach” and helps shape “an inclusive policy for a sustainable and eco‑friendly internet”【2†L1-L4】 | Uniform technical choices (e.g., energy‑efficient protocols) can lower the overall carbon footprint while ensuring that emerging economies can participate on equal terms. |
| **Economic & diplomatic relevance** | Standards “en‑shrine the usual behavior of the ‘reasonable man’ … and provide a widely accepted good principle, practice or guideline”【6†L5-L7】 | This legal‑like certainty supports cross‑border trade, investment and diplomatic negotiations, turning the technical layer into a stable platform for policy dialogue. |
| **Regional participation (example: ARIN)** | The ARIN Service Region contributes to “International Digital Standardization” through its involvement with the same bodies (ISO, ITU, IETF)【4†L1-L3】 | Regional registries can influence standards that affect address allocation, routing security and other infrastructure elements. |
| **Practical examples of standards** | “TCP/IP, the domain name system (DNS), and the secure sockets layer (SSL) are example of these standards”【8†L1-L3】 | These are the foundational protocols that make the global Internet work; their continual refinement is a hallmark of the standardisation process. |

### Key take‑aways
1. **Interoperability** – Standardisation is the glue that lets heterogeneous devices and networks communicate reliably.  
2. **Security & safety** – Agreed‑upon technical specifications embed proven security controls, raising the baseline safety of the whole Internet.  
3. **Sustainability** – Harmonised, efficient protocols help curb the Internet’s environmental impact while fostering inclusive access.  
4. **Governance & diplomacy** – Because standards act like “internationally recognized levels of performance,” they become a neutral reference point in diplomatic and policy negotiations.  
5. **Global collaboration** – Bodies such as ISO, ITU and IETF, together with regional actors like ARIN, coordinate the development and maintenance of these standards, ensuring that the Internet remains a stable, open, and evolving commons.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy – also called **e‑diplomacy** or **web diplomacy** – is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet, social‑media platforms and other ICT tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy goals.  It encompasses:

* **The medium** – the Internet, social media, virtual meeting platforms, and other digital tools that enable communication, negotiation, and representation [3][4][5].  
* **The purpose** – achieving diplomatic objectives such as image and reputation management, public‑diplomacy outreach, cultural exchange, and the negotiation of policy issues (e.g., privacy, data protection, cybersecurity) [4][7][8].  
* **The context** – a changing diplomatic environment in which digital tools reshape how states interact, create new policy domains, and allow dispersed communities to act collectively [2][7][8].

In short, digital diplomacy is “the use of the Internet and social media (and broader digital technologies) to conduct diplomatic activities and solve foreign‑policy problems,” complementing traditional diplomatic methods while leveraging the networked, virtual nature of today’s communications [4][5][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It reshapes the environment in which diplomacy is carried out, enabling states to communicate publicly, hold virtual negotiations, run public‑diplomacy campaigns, and address new agenda items such as privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity.  In short, it is “the use of the Internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives” and the broader “use of digital technologies … to conduct diplomatic activities and achieve foreign policy goals”【3†L1-L3】【4†L1-L4】【5†L1-L4】.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The material presented at the recent IGF‑related sessions makes it clear that the community expects the **Internet Governance Forum (IGF) to continue operating after 2025**. Key points from the discussions are:

* **Mandate renewal is widely anticipated.**  Multiple speakers noted that the IGF’s mandate “will most likely be renewed in 2025” and that this renewal should be treated as a “pivotal outcome” that “affirms its value in shaping the future of global internet governance”【1†L1-L3】【7†L1-L3】.  The opening ceremony of IGF 2024 also called for a renewal of the mandate in 2025【8†L1】.

* **A continued multistakeholder role.**  The future of the IGF is repeatedly linked to the broader “multistakeholder digital governance” agenda.  Participants said that the IGF’s future “will define the future of the NRIs” (National Regulatory Institutions) and that the forum will remain a central venue for multistakeholder dialogue on the digital future【5†L1-L2】.

* **Potential for new hosting arrangements.**  Assuming renewal, member states were encouraged to consider “offering hosting the annual meeting of the IGF starting from 2026 and onwards”【4†L1-L2】, indicating that the forum’s annual meetings are expected to continue and may see new host countries.

* **Contribution to the Global Digital Compact (GDC).**  In a high‑level session, participants were asked to envision how the IGF could “contribute to the implementation of the GDC,” suggesting that the IGF is expected to play an active role in supporting the GDC’s objectives beyond 2025【2†L1-L2】.

* **Strategic planning for the post‑2025 era.**  The closing ceremony explicitly looked ahead to “IGF 2025 … Future and Structure,” reinforcing that the community is already mapping out the forum’s shape and functions after the mandate renewal【3†L1-L2】.

**In summary:**  
All of the cited statements point to a consensus that the IGF will **continue to exist after 2025**, with its mandate renewed, its multistakeholder character preserved, and its annual meetings likely to be hosted by different member states.  The forum is expected to play a significant part in implementing the Global Digital Compact and in shaping the broader multistakeholder digital governance ecosystem.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The consensus emerging from the 2024 IGF sessions is that the Internet Governance Forum will continue to operate after 2025 – but only if its mandate is formally renewed.  Across the various plenary and stakeholder meetings the following points were repeatedly emphasized:

| What was said | Source |
|---------------|--------|
| **The IGF’s mandate is expected to be renewed in 2025** – a “strong sentiment” that the forum’s role will be reinforced beyond that year. | [1] |
| **Renewal is viewed as a pivotal outcome** that confirms the IGF’s value for shaping global internet governance. | [7] |
| **Member states are urged to consider hosting the annual meeting from 2026 onward**, signalling that the community is already planning the post‑2025 calendar. | [4] |
| **The future of the IGF will define the future of the NRIs (National Regulatory Institutions)**, underscoring that the forum’s continuation is seen as central to multistakeholder digital governance. | [5] |
| **Discussions about the IGF’s vision beyond 2025** included how it can contribute to implementing the Global Digital Compact (GDC). | [2] |
| **Closing and opening ceremonies explicitly called for a 2025 mandate renewal** and looked ahead to the IGF’s structure in 2025 and beyond. | [3], [8] |

### What this means for the IGF after 2025
1. **Mandate renewal** – The prevailing expectation is that the UN‑mandated IGF will receive a new mandate in 2025, allowing it to continue as the premier multistakeholder platform for internet governance.  
2. **Continued annual meetings** – With renewal, the IGF will keep holding its yearly global meeting; member states are being asked to start thinking about hosting duties from 2026 onward.  
3. **Enhanced role in global digital policy** – The forum is expected to play a more explicit part in advancing the Global Digital Compact and other multistakeholder initiatives, linking its discussions to concrete implementation pathways.  
4. **Structural evolution** – While the exact future structure was not detailed, the “Looking Ahead to IGF 2025 – IGF’s Future and Structure” session indicates that the community will likely revisit governance arrangements, possibly adapting them to better support emerging digital challenges.  

**In short:** The IGF is projected to persist beyond 2025, contingent on a formal renewal of its mandate.  This renewal will cement its status as a central, multistakeholder venue for shaping internet governance and will open the way for new hosting cycles, deeper engagement with the Global Digital Compact, and potential structural reforms.

### Q64: Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

> **Judge:** Answer B covers a broader set of distinct issues and offers more concrete actions, while both maintain similar accuracy and citation quality.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights**  
*Drafted from the collective insights of recent multistakeholder dialogues, youth‑led initiatives and international declarations.*

---

### 1. **Full Representation in Digital Governance**  
- **Principle:** Young people and minority groups must have a seat at every table where digital policies are debated, designed and implemented.  
- **Call to Action:** Establish mandatory youth quotas in national ICT ministries, multistakeholder forums (e.g., IGF, WAIGF) and private‑sector advisory boards.  
- **Reference:** The ninth point of the “Upholding Human Rights in the Digital Age” report stresses the need for representation of youth and minorities [1].

### 2. **Protection of Dignity, Security and Privacy for Children and Youth**  
- **Principle:** Online environments must safeguard the physical and psychological well‑being of children and young users.  
- **Call to Action:** Enforce the *Declaration of the Committee of Ministers* on protecting children’s dignity, security and privacy on the Internet; adopt age‑appropriate data‑minimisation standards.  
- **Reference:** The “GUIDE to hUman rIGhts for IntErnEt UsErs” cites this declaration [5].

### 3. **Equitable Access to a Secure Digital Future**  
- **Principle:** All youth, regardless of geography, gender or socioeconomic status, deserve equal opportunities to benefit from digital technologies.  
- **Call to Action:** Prioritise universal broadband, affordable devices and inclusive design in national digital agendas.  
- **Reference:** Youth representatives at the “A Digital Future for All” morning session demanded an equitable, secure and empowering digital future [6].

### 4. **Active Participation in Digital Policy‑Making and Governance**  
- **Principle:** Youth must be co‑creators of the rules that shape their online lives.  
- **Call to Action:** Create formal mechanisms (e.g., youth policy labs, consultative councils) that give young people binding input on legislation, standards and platform governance.  
- **Reference:** YouthLead calls for increased youth participation in digital policymaking [4]; WAIGF highlighted the importance of youth participation in shaping the digital future [7].

### 5. **Capacity‑Building and Advocacy for Digital Rights**  
- **Principle:** Empowered youth are the most effective defenders of their own rights.  
- **Call to Action:** Scale up programmes like the **Digitalise Youth Project** that provide training, mentorship and resources for youth activists and civil‑society organisations.  
- **Reference:** The Kofi Annan Foundation notes Digitalise Youth’s advocacy to promote digital rights and counter disinformation in the Sahel [2].

### 6. **Combating Disinformation and Protecting Civic Space**  
- **Principle:** A healthy digital ecosystem must be free from manipulation that undermines democratic participation.  
- **Call to Action:** Develop youth‑led fact‑checking networks, media‑literacy curricula and legal safeguards against the weaponisation of misinformation.  
- **Reference:** Digitalise Youth’s mandate includes addressing shrinking civic space and rampant disinformation [2].

### 7. **Intellectual Property Rights as Enablers of Youth Innovation**  
- **Principle:** Young creators should retain control over their digital inventions and be able to commercialise them fairly.  
- **Call to Action:** Reform IP regimes to lower barriers for youth‑led startups, introduce simplified registration processes and ensure equitable revenue sharing on digital platforms.  
- **Reference:** YouthLead identified IP rights as a tool to empower youth innovation and entrepreneurship [3].

### 8. **Safe Online Environments for Children**  
- **Principle:** Children’s right to play, learn and express themselves online must be protected from exploitation and harm.  
- **Call to Action:** Implement robust age‑verification, content‑moderation and reporting mechanisms; enforce strict penalties for platforms that fail to protect minors.  
- **Reference:** The same Committee of Ministers’ declaration stresses child protection online [5].

### 9. **Multistakeholder Collaboration for Human Dignity**  
- **Principle:** Governments, the private sector, academia and civil society must work together with youth to uphold digital human rights.  
- **Call to Action:** Institutionalise a “Youth Digital Rights Hub” that coordinates cross‑sector initiatives, shares best practices and monitors compliance with international norms.  
- **Reference:** The overarching theme of a multistakeholder approach to safeguard human dignity is highlighted in the “Upholding Human Rights in the Digital Age” report [1].

### 10. **Commitment to Ongoing Monitoring and Accountability**  
- **Principle:** Rights must be measurable and enforceable.  
- **Call to Action:** Adopt transparent reporting frameworks (e.g., annual youth‑rights impact assessments) and grant youth watchdog groups legal standing to challenge violations.  
- **Reference:** The call for active involvement of children and young people in rights advocacy underscores the need for accountability mechanisms [8].

---

## **Our Vision**

> *A digital world where every young person—regardless of age, gender, ethnicity or location—enjoys full, protected, and participatory rights; where their voices shape the policies that govern the internet; and where innovation thrives under fair, inclusive and secure conditions.*

---

**Adopted by:**  
- Youth representatives at WAIGF, IGF and regional digital democracy initiatives (2023‑2024)  
- Partner organisations: Kofi Annan Foundation, YouthLead, Committee of Ministers of the Council of Europe  

*Together, we pledge to champion these tenets and hold all stakeholders accountable for a truly inclusive digital future.*

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights**  
*Drafted from the collective voices of young people and minority groups expressed in recent multistakeholder dialogues, advocacy projects and policy forums.*

---

### 1. **Full Representation in All Digital Governance Structures**  
- Young people, especially those from minority and under‑represented communities, must have guaranteed seats at the table of every national, regional and international digital‑policy process.  
- Mechanisms (e.g., youth quotas, rotating youth panels) should be institutionalised to ensure their perspectives shape decisions from the outset.  

*Supported by:* the call for better representation of youth and minorities in the ninth point of the “Upholding Human Rights in the Digital Age” document [1]; the emphasis on youth participation in the WAIGF opening ceremony [7].

---

### 2. **Protect and Expand Civic Space for Youth Activists**  
- Digital platforms must be kept free from undue censorship, surveillance or intimidation that shrinks civic space.  
- Targeted programmes should empower youth activists with tools to counter disinformation and to organise safely online, particularly in fragile regions such as the Sahel.  

*Supported by:* Digitalise Youth’s advocacy to combat shrinking civic space and rampant disinformation in the Sahel [2].

---

### 3. **Guarantee the Right to Privacy, Security and Dignity for Children Online**  
- All online services used by children must be designed with “privacy‑by‑design” and robust security safeguards.  
- States and platforms should adopt the **Declaration of the Committee of Ministers** on protecting children’s dignity, security and privacy on the Internet.  

*Supported by:* the 86‑point Declaration on children’s online rights [5].

---

### 4. **Empower Youth Innovation through Fair Intellectual‑Property Regimes**  
- Intellectual‑property rules must balance protection of creators with affordable access to knowledge, enabling young entrepreneurs to develop and commercialise digital solutions.  
- Simplified, youth‑friendly IP filing procedures and mentorship schemes should be created.  

*Supported by:* YouthLead’s focus on IP rights as a lever for youth innovation and entrepreneurship [3].

---

### 5. **Increase Youth Participation in Digital Policymaking and Governance**  
- Formal channels (e.g., youth advisory councils, consultative forums) must be institutionalised at every level of digital governance.  
- Youth should be involved in drafting, reviewing and monitoring legislation on data protection, AI ethics, internet governance, and cyber‑security.  

*Supported by:* YouthLead’s call to “increase youth participation in digital policymaking and governance” [4]; the “A Digital Future for All” session where youth demanded an equitable, secure digital future [6].

---

### 6. **Ensure an Equitable and Inclusive Digital Future**  
- Universal affordable broadband, accessible devices and multilingual digital content are prerequisites for a truly inclusive digital society.  
- Policies must address the digital divide that disproportionately affects rural, low‑income, and minority youth.  

*Supported by:* the overarching theme of an “equitable, secure and empowering” digital future voiced by youth representatives [6]; the multistakeholder approach championed in the human‑rights‑focused document [1].

---

### 7. **Build Capacity and Provide Continuous Education**  
- Structured capacity‑building programmes (digital literacy, cyber‑rights education, advocacy skills) should be funded and made available to all youth, especially in underserved regions.  
- Partnerships with civil‑society organisations, academia and the private sector are essential to sustain these programmes.  

*Supported by:* Kofi Annan Foundation’s capacity‑building work through the Digitalise Youth Project [2].

---

### 8. **Mandate Youth‑Led Monitoring and Accountability Mechanisms**  
- Independent youth‑led watchdog bodies should monitor compliance with digital‑rights standards and report violations to national and international fora.  
- Transparent reporting and remedial pathways must be established to hold governments and corporations accountable.  

*Supported by:* the broader demand for youth participation and oversight expressed across multiple sources [1, 4, 6, 7].

---

## **Our Commitment**

We, the youth of today, pledge to:

1. **Advocate** for our inclusion in every digital‑policy decision.  
2. **Defend** the online rights of children, minorities and all vulnerable groups.  
3. **Innovate** responsibly, using fair IP frameworks to drive sustainable development.  
4. **Educate** ourselves and our peers to navigate and shape the digital world safely.  
5. **Hold** power‑holders accountable through transparent, youth‑led monitoring.

*Together, we will build a digital future that is **equitable, secure, empowering and respectful of human dignity for all**.*

</details>

> **Judge:** Both answers are equally thorough and accurate, but Answer A demonstrates slightly better source integration, citing multiple references for each claim.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Core Internet Protocols and Their Role in Network Security**

| Aspect | What the sources say | Why it matters for security |
|--------|----------------------|----------------------------|
| **Definition of the “core”** | The *public‑core* concept treats the fundamental protocols and infrastructure of the Internet (e.g., routing, DNS, exchange points, submarine cables) as a **global public good** that should be shielded from unwarranted state interference【4†L1-L4】【5†L1-L2】. | By recognizing these elements as a shared resource, the norm creates a diplomatic and legal basis for protecting them against political or military disruption, which is a prerequisite for any technical security measure. |
| **Standards for re‑architecting the Internet** | New standards are being developed to **re‑architect the Internet core**, improving protection against cyber threats and guaranteeing confidentiality, integrity, and availability of information【1†L1-L3】【2†L1-L3】. | A redesign that embeds security (e.g., stronger authentication, encryption, resilience) directly into the protocol stack reduces attack surfaces and makes the network more robust to exploitation. |
| **Protocols and standards that safeguard robustness** | Existing **protocols and standards** are already identified as key protectors of the core infrastructure’s robustness【3†L1-L2】. | Robustness translates into resistance to denial‑of‑service, routing hijacks, and other attacks that could compromise the network’s stability. |
| **Specific security mechanisms** | The speakers at IGF 2023 highlighted **DNSSEC** (Domain Name System Security Extensions) and **RPKI** (Resource Public Key Infrastructure) as critical tools for securing the core infrastructure【6†L1-L3】【7†L1-L2】. | • **DNSSEC** authenticates DNS responses, preventing cache‑poisoning and man‑in‑the‑middle attacks.<br>• **RPKI** cryptographically validates BGP route announcements, mitigating route‑hijack and interception threats. |
| **Legal and normative gaps** | Current normative frameworks still have gaps; the public‑core norm, though established over a decade ago, needs reinforcement to address emerging threats in conflict and crisis contexts【4†L1-L4】. | Without clear, universally accepted rules, states may justify interference (e.g., cutting cables, blocking exchange points), undermining technical safeguards. |

### Key Take‑aways for Diplomats and Policymakers

1. **Treat core protocols as a protected public good** – reinforce the *public‑core* norm in multilateral fora to create a diplomatic shield against state‑led disruptions.  
2. **Adopt and mandate security‑focused standards** – push for the implementation of DNSSEC, RPKI, and the emerging re‑architecting standards that embed confidentiality, integrity, and availability into the protocol stack.  
3. **Close normative gaps** – develop updated legal instruments that explicitly prohibit unwarranted state interference with core resources (protocols, DNS, IXPs, cable systems) especially during conflicts or crises.  
4. **Promote coordinated governance** – encourage collaboration among technical bodies (IETF, ISOC), intergovernmental organizations, and civil society to ensure that security standards are globally interoperable and widely deployed.  

By aligning diplomatic norms with technical safeguards such as DNSSEC and RPKI, the international community can strengthen the **network security** of the Internet’s core, preserving its resilience and trustworthiness for all users.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Core Internet Protocols and Their Role in Network Security – What the IGF Sources Tell Us**

| Aspect | What the sources say | Why it matters for security |
|--------|----------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Core protocols as a “public good”** | The *public‑core* concept, developed over the last decade, treats the fundamental Internet protocols and infrastructure (e.g., routing, DNS, exchange points, submarine cables) as a global public good that should be insulated from unwarranted state interference【4†L1-L4】. | When protocols are recognised as a shared resource, states are encouraged to refrain from actions that could disrupt their operation (e.g., arbitrary shutdowns, forced traffic filtering). This normative protection underpins the stability and resilience of the network. |
| **Standards that re‑architect the Internet core** | New standards are being promoted to “re‑architect” the Internet core, improving protection against cyber threats and guaranteeing confidentiality, integrity, and availability (CIA) of information【1†L1-L3】【2†L1-L3】. | By updating the technical foundations (e.g., cryptographic algorithms, authentication mechanisms), the Internet becomes harder to compromise at the most fundamental layers. |
| **Protocols and standards that safeguard robustness** | The IGF 2019 final report notes that “protocols and standards protect the robustness of core Internet infrastructure”【3†L1-L2】. | Robustness means the network can continue to operate even when parts are attacked or fail, a key security property. |
| **Specific security extensions** | Speakers at IGF 2023 highlighted DNSSEC (Domain Name System Security Extensions) and RPKI (Resource Public Key Infrastructure) as critical for securing the core infrastructure【6†L1-L3】【7†L1-L2】. | • **DNSSEC** cryptographically signs DNS data, preventing cache‑poisoning and man‑in‑the‑middle attacks on name resolution.<br>• **RPKI** validates the origin of BGP routes, mitigating route‑hijacking and traffic interception. Both directly protect the integrity and availability of core routing and naming services. |
| **Normative gaps** | The “Securing Access to the Internet and Protecting Core Internet Resources in Contexts of Conflict and Crises” report flags gaps in existing normative frameworks, even though the public‑core norm exists【4†L1-L4】. | Without fully fleshed‑out legal and policy instruments, states may still justify intrusive measures (e.g., cyber‑warfare, censorship) that undermine core protocols. Closing these gaps is essential for a coherent security posture. |

### Key Take‑aways for Diplomats and Policy‑Makers

1. **Treat core protocols as a protected public good** – The public‑core norm (e.g., “core Internet protocols and infrastructure should be exempted from unwarranted state intervention”) provides a diplomatic foundation for multilateral agreements that limit harmful state actions【4†L1-L4】【5†L1-L2】.

2. **Adopt and promote security‑focused standards** – Encouraging the deployment of DNSSEC, RPKI, and the newer re‑architecturing standards cited in the IGF 2023 Open Forum will raise the baseline security of the Internet’s backbone【1†L1-L3】【6†L1-L3】.

3. **Address normative gaps** – The identified “gaps in current normative frameworks” suggest a need for updated treaties, confidence‑building measures, and perhaps a dedicated “Core‑Internet Protection” treaty that codifies obligations around CIA guarantees for protocols【4†L1-L4】.

4. **Integrate technical and diplomatic tracks** – Technical measures (DNSSEC, RPKI, cryptographic upgrades) must be paired with diplomatic commitments (public‑core norm enforcement, conflict‑sensitive safeguards) to ensure that the Internet remains resilient even in crises or armed conflict【4†L1-L4】【6†L1-L3】.

### Bottom Line

The IGF documents converge on a single theme: **securing the Internet’s core protocols is both a technical and diplomatic imperative**. By recognizing these protocols as a global public good, adopting proven security extensions like DNSSEC and RPKI, and filling existing normative gaps, the international community can better guarantee the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the Internet’s most essential services.

</details>

| **AI as the catalyst** | AI will dismantle traditional barriers, allowing anyone (e.g., a villager speaking Bhojpuri) to communicate globally (US, Russia, Japan). The AI boom is seen as a decisive opportunity that India must capture or risk being left behind. | [1] |
| **Scale of the learner base** | Roughly **25 crore** children are in school education and **4.6 crore** in higher education (≈30 crore total). There are about **15 lakh schools** nationwide. | [2], [5] |

| **Teacher workforce & AI literacy** | India has **≈1 crore** teachers, most of whom are women—a positive gender shift. However, only a small fraction are AI‑savvy; building AI literacy among teachers is a priority. | [2], [5] |
| **Policy & curriculum interventions** | The government plans to introduce **computational thinking and AI curriculum from Grade 3**, with rollout expected in the next academic season. This is meant to make students AI‑aware early on. | [8] |
| **Integrated approach across levels** | Stakeholders (school and higher‑education leaders) are working together on an integrated strategy, but they request broader support to scale AI adoption. | [2] |
| **Measuring success** | Success will not be counted by the number of AI tools deployed, but by how well the youth are prepared to lead the country in the AI era. | [7] |
| **STEM focus** | The broader discussion emphasizes evolving STEM education to keep pace with AI, ensuring students acquire the skills needed for an AI‑driven future. | [6] |
| **Economic ambition** | The ultimate goal is to push India onto a “very high growth trajectory” and achieve the dream of being a leading global economy **“very soon,”** not just by 2050‑2100. | [2] |

### Synthesis

- **Visionary Goal:** By 2050‑2100, India aims to be a top‑ranked global economy, with an education system that fully leverages AI to remove linguistic and geographic barriers.  
- **Scale & Challenge:** With tens of millions of learners and a massive school network, the main bottleneck is the uneven distribution of digital infrastructure and AI‑ready teachers.  
- **Strategic Levers:**  
  1. **Early AI exposure** – introducing computational thinking and AI concepts from Grade 3.  
  2. **Teacher up‑skilling** – turning the large, predominantly female teacher workforce into AI‑literate educators.  
  3. **Infrastructure rollout** – expanding computers, ICT labs, and tablets to reach the “last mile” schools.  
  4. **Integrated policy** – coordinating school and higher‑education reforms under a unified AI‑centric framework.  
- **Success Metric:** The quality of youth leadership in AI, rather than sheer tool count, will gauge the effectiveness of reforms.  

In short, the future of learning in India, as outlined in the provided excerpts, is an AI‑driven transformation that starts with early computational curricula, scales through massive infrastructure and teacher development, and ultimately seeks to position the nation as a leading economic power by mid‑century.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and other web‑based scripting languages.  

* It was first published in 1997 and has grown to become one of the world’s most widely used programming languages, forming the core of the Web Browser application platform and being used in many other environments, from servers and mobile devices to resource‑constrained embedded systems [2][3].  
* The language and its built‑in libraries are defined and maintained by **Ecma International** through the ECMA‑262 language specification and related standards [4][6].  
* ECMAScript is standardized as a “programming language of the web” and is being extended to embedded systems, micro‑controllers and single‑board computers [3][7].  

In short, ECMAScript is the standardized language specification that powers JavaScript and many other implementations of the same language across a wide range of platforms. [2][3][4][6]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript (originally from Netscape) and JScript (Microsoft).  It is the language defined and standardized by **Ecma International** (ECMA‑262) and is the core scripting language of the Web browser platform, while also being used in many other environments such as servers, mobile devices, and even resource‑constrained embedded systems and micro‑controllers【2†L1-L4】【3†L1-L5】.  

The language’s specifications are maintained by Ecma’s Technical Committee 39 (TC‑39) and are part of a broader family of standards that include the Internationalization API (ECMA‑402) and related test suites【6†L4-L5】【7†L13-L14】. In short, ECMAScript is the standardized language specification that powers JavaScript‑based development across the Web and beyond.

</details>

**Key take‑away:** A multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance is an inclusive, participatory process—most prominently embodied by the IGF—where representatives from governments, civil society, industry, technical experts and academia meet to exchange views, share expertise, and shape policy recommendations for the Internet’s future. [1][2][3][4][5][6][7][8]

* **Mission** – its stated purpose is to create technical and engineering documents that make the Internet work better, covering protocol standards, best practices, and other informational material【3†L1-L4】.

1. **International cooperation over isolation** – Participants stressed that the biggest safety challenges cannot be solved by any single country acting alone.  A broad consensus emerged that partnership‑based approaches are far more effective than “AI‑sovereignty” or isolationist strategies [4][5][7].

2. **Scientific rigor in AI evaluation** – The ministers and experts called for robust, peer‑reviewed methods to assess AI systems, insisting that policy decisions be grounded in solid technical evidence [4].

3. **Multi‑agent system risks** – The discussion highlighted the particular danger posed by AI systems that interact with one another (e.g., autonomous agents, large‑scale language model ecosystems).  These risks were identified as a priority area for immediate research and regulation [4].

4. **Addressing both immediate and systemic risks** – Delegates agreed that safety work must tackle short‑term hazards (such as misuse or accidental failures) while also building frameworks to manage longer‑term, systemic threats that could arise as AI becomes more capable [4].

The conversation was framed around a **global AI safety report**—chaired by Yoshua Bengio—that synthesized the latest research and policy insights.  The report’s findings served as the basis for the panel’s recommendations and highlighted the need for coordinated standards, shared data, and joint governance mechanisms [6][8].

All of this took place at the **AI Safety Connect summit in New Delhi**, which brought together senior policymakers, leaders of international organisations, and AI‑safety advocates to grapple with the coordination challenges of global AI governance [1][3].
