| **Digital transformation & public‑service competence** | • Implement a broad set of measures – especially competence‑enhancement programmes and the development of digital public services – that both address the immediate crisis and leave Norway in a stronger position once the crisis is over.<br>• Revise regulations to remove barriers and ensure clarity and consistency across agencies (the “One Digital Public Sector” strategy 2019‑2025). | 【4】, 【6】 |
| **International development & innovation** | • Roll out a new **Digital Innovation Programme** and a **Digitalisation for Development** programme that share digital know‑how with partner countries and build their capacity.<br>• Intensify support for local incubators and innovation centres, both through existing strategic partnerships and via participation in the World Bank’s **infoDev** programme that promotes entrepreneurship.<br>• Continue to promote digitalisation within multilateral organisations (World Bank, UN agencies, etc.) as part of Norway’s broader development policy. | 【5】, 【3】 |

**In short, Norway’s post‑COVID agenda combines:**

1. **A swift removal of pandemic‑related economic restrictions** to allow the economy to restructure and grow sustainably.  
2. **A strong push for digitalisation and skills development** within the public sector, ensuring that digital public services and regulatory frameworks are modernised and coherent.  
3. **An outward‑looking digital development strategy**, using Norway’s own digital expertise to support partner countries, foster entrepreneurship, and deepen cooperation with multilateral bodies.  

These measures are intended to both mitigate the lingering effects of the pandemic and position Norway for long‑term competitiveness, green growth, and high employment.

</details>

| **Digital transformation & competence building** | • Use competence‑enhancement programmes and the development of digital public services – originally introduced to cope with the crisis – as lasting assets that will leave Norway stronger after the pandemic.<br>• Treat the digital strategy for development policy as a “living strategy” that is continuously refined based on new knowledge, project results, partner feedback and civil‑society input.<br>• Revise regulations to remove barriers and create clearer, more consistent rules across public agencies (One Digital Public Sector strategy). | [4]; [2]; [6] |

**In short**, Norway’s post‑COVID policy combines:

1. **Removal of pandemic‑related economic restrictions** to enable a swift restructuring of the economy.  
2. **A focus on green, competitive, and sustainable growth** (competitiveness, emissions cuts, green growth, strong labour market).  
3. **A lasting digital agenda** that leverages the crisis‑driven boost in digital public services, competence building, regulatory simplification, and international digital cooperation.  

These measures are intended to both address the immediate fallout of the pandemic and to position Norway for a stronger, more digital‑enabled future.

### Q82: What are key disagreements in the UN Cybercrime Convention?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 6.11s | 5.89s (+0.12s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 1 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A addresses a broader set of disagreement points with concrete examples and richer citations, making it more complete and specific while remaining accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The negotiations on a new United Nations cyber‑crime treaty have repeatedly stalled because delegations cannot agree on a handful of core issues.  Across the excerpts the following points emerge as the **principal points of contention**:

| Area of disagreement | What the states cannot agree on | Why it matters |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------|----------------|
| **Scope of the treaty** | Whether the instrument should cover only “serious crimes” or be broadened to allow data‑collection for any offense defined under domestic law.  The draft’s language is vague, creating the risk that states could invoke the treaty to pursue low‑level or politically‑motivated cases. | Ambiguity could lead to inconsistent application worldwide and undermine the treaty’s credibility.  (see [2]) |
| **Cross‑border surveillance & data‑sharing powers** | The extent to which the treaty permits real‑time data collection, interception of communications, and other intrusive investigative techniques without strong safeguards.  Some states push for expansive powers; others demand tighter limits. | Unchecked surveillance threatens privacy and could erode civil liberties if not bounded by clear rules. (see [1]) |
| **Human‑rights safeguards** | Lack of mandatory protections such as prior judicial authorization, transparency of data access, and enforceable oversight mechanisms.  The EFF’s Katitza Rodriguez stresses that without these, safeguards will fall to national law rather than an international standard. | Without robust safeguards, the treaty could lower global human‑rights and privacy standards, contrary to existing international norms. (see [1]) |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | Concerns that a new UN convention might conflict with the Budapest Convention and other regional agreements that already set privacy and procedural safeguards. | Incompatibility could create a fragmented legal landscape and weaken cooperation. (see [4]) |
| **Disparities in privacy protection** | Different countries have varying levels of privacy law; the draft does not harmonise these protections, leading to “disparities in privacy protections across different countries.” | This unevenness may discourage cooperation and raise fears of “forum shopping” for the weakest safeguards. (see [6]) |
| **International‑cooperation mechanisms** | The section on how states cooperate in investigations and evidence‑sharing is contested, especially regarding the procedural safeguards that should accompany such cooperation. | Overly permissive cooperation could bypass domestic due‑process guarantees. (see [5]) |

In summary, the **key disagreements** revolve around **(1) the treaty’s scope and definition of cyber‑crime**, **(2) the breadth of surveillance and data‑collection powers**, **(3) the presence (or absence) of strong human‑rights and judicial safeguards**, **(4) ensuring compatibility with existing regional frameworks**, and **(5) how international cooperation will be structured without compromising privacy**. These unresolved issues have repeatedly prevented states from reaching a final, consensus‑based agreement.  

**Sources**: [1] UN Cybercrime Convention – lack of consensus on scope, human‑rights safeguards, intrusive powers; [2] Comparative analysis – ambiguity of scope and “serious crimes” definition; [4] Risks and opportunities – incompatibility with existing instruments; [5] Countries clash – contentious cooperation section; [6] Main Session – inadequate safeguards and privacy disparities.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The negotiations have repeatedly stalled around a handful of “deal‑breakers” that fall into three broad clusters:

| Area of disagreement | What the states cannot agree on | Why it matters |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------|----------------|
| **Scope of the treaty and cross‑border surveillance** | • How broadly the convention should apply to “serious crimes” and whether it can be used to justify real‑time data collection or communication interception for any offense that a domestic law defines as cyber‑related. <br>• The extent of mandatory cross‑border cooperation in surveillance and evidence‑sharing. | A vague scope could let states use the treaty to pursue low‑level or politically motivated cases, while a narrow scope may limit the ability to combat truly trans‑national cyber threats. |
| **Human‑rights safeguards** | • Whether the treaty must embed **prior judicial authorization**, **transparency** and **strong, mandatory human‑rights protections** for any data‑access or interception measures. <br>• The level of protection for privacy, freedom of expression and due‑process guarantees. | Without clear safeguards, the convention could erode global privacy standards and enable intrusive state surveillance that conflicts with existing human‑rights norms. |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | • Whether the new UN text can coexist with the **Budapest Convention** and other regional agreements, or whether it will create a “lower‑standard” regime that undermines those instruments. <br>• How the convention’s cooperation provisions will align with current international law on evidence‑sharing and mutual legal assistance. | A treaty that diverges from the Budapest Convention could lead to a fragmented global regime, weakening overall cyber‑crime enforcement and creating legal uncertainty for states and private actors. |

These points are repeatedly highlighted in the negotiation reports:

* The draft’s **red‑highlighted provisions** show a lack of consensus on scope, human‑rights safeguards, and intrusive powers such as real‑time data collection or communication interception without strong mandatory protections [1].  
* Experts note that **“issues with its scope of cross‑border surveillance cooperation and human‑rights protections remain unresolved”**, raising fears of a decline in global privacy standards [1][5].  
* The **ambiguity around the scope of data collection for any offense defined by domestic law** could produce inconsistent application across jurisdictions [2].  
* Concerns are also voiced that the UN treaty **might be incompatible with existing instruments** like the Budapest Convention [4][6].

In short, the key disagreements revolve around **how far the treaty can reach (its scope), how it protects fundamental rights (human‑rights safeguards), and whether it will mesh with or undercut the existing cyber‑crime framework**. Until these gaps are bridged, a final, globally‑binding UN Cybercrime Convention remains elusive.

</details>

Plenary discussions are the full‑assembly deliberations that take place during a meeting’s plenary sessions.  In the context provided they:

* **Involve all member states** – the discussions are open to the entire membership rather than a limited working group [8].
* **Begin with a careful review of the agenda** – the principle that proceedings start with a thorough scrutiny of the agenda is highlighted as “central to the discussions” [1].
* **Address substantive issues from the agenda** – for example, they examine the pillars and other elements of paragraph 1 of General Assembly resolution 75/240 [2].
* **Integrate inputs from thematic groups** – the thematic‑group work is meant to “enrich the discussions in the plenary session” [6].
* **Lead to decisions and outcomes** – the plenary discussions “discuss the pillars and decide” and are used to present outcomes such as interim reports [2], [7].
* **Link to capacity‑building and resilience** – the plenary also covers topics like capacity building that improve resilience and resource mobilisation [8].

Thus, plenary discussions are the comprehensive, member‑state‑wide debates that review agenda items, incorporate thematic‑group findings, and produce decisions and reports on substantive issues.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Plenary discussions are the full‑member‑state meetings that bring together every participant in the forum to review and decide on the items placed on the agenda. In this context they are used to:

* Begin with a thorough scrutiny of the agenda and institutional dialogue topics [1].  
* Debate the “pillars” and substantive issues set out in General Assembly resolution 75/240, especially under agenda item 5, and reach decisions [2].  
* Integrate inputs from thematic groups and negotiation‑track work so that the plenary can be enriched by those earlier discussions [6].  
* Present interim reports (including checking for errors such as spelling mistakes) to the whole body [7].  
* Involve **all** member states, with a focus on capacity‑building measures that improve resilience and resource mobilisation [8].

Thus, plenary discussions serve as the central, inclusive deliberative forum where agenda items are examined, thematic inputs are synthesized, and collective decisions are made.

</details>

### Q85: Youth Voices for Digital Rights

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more facets, provides a detailed table and concrete recommendations, and consistently cites sources, making it more complete and specific.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Why Youth Voices Matter for Digital Rights**

1. **Driving More Equitable Digital Policies**  
   - Youth participation helps ensure that digital policies reflect the lived realities of the next‑generation users, making them *more equitable and effective* [1].  

2. **Highlighting Unique Vulnerabilities**  
   - Young people experience specific risks in the digital age—such as data‑driven exploitation, privacy breaches, and exposure to disinformation—and can articulate these gaps better than any other stakeholder [1][7][8].

3. **Advocating for Child‑Centred AI and Data Design**  
   - Initiatives like the *WS #376 “Elevating Children’s Voices in AI Design”* bring youth NGOs (e.g., Youth for Privacy) to the table, pushing for AI systems that respect children’s privacy and rights [3].

4. **Protecting Children’s Rights Online**  
   - High‑level sessions on *“Protecting Children’s Rights in the Digital World”* stress that youth must have the tools and opportunities to shape solutions that safeguard their online safety [4].

5. **Combating Shrinking Civic Space & Disinformation**  
   - Projects such as the *Digitalise Youth Project* (part of the Kofi Annan Foundation’s Digital Democracy Initiative) empower local youth activists to advocate for digital rights, counter misinformation, and expand civic participation in the Sahel and neighboring regions [5].

6. **Building Capacity for Ongoing Advocacy**  
   - Beyond one‑off events, capacity‑building work (e.g., through Digitalise Youth) equips young people with the skills to sustain long‑term digital‑rights advocacy at local, regional, and international levels [5].

---

### How Youth Voices Are Being Amplified

| Platform / Initiative | Youth Role | Core Contribution |
|-----------------------|------------|--------------------|
| **WS #32 – Harnessing Youth Voices to Transform the Data Economy** | Participants, speakers, and co‑designers | Advocate for rights, highlight digital‑age vulnerabilities, push for inclusive data‑governance [1][6][7][8] |
| **Youth for Privacy (NGO)** | Representative Joon Baek speaks on privacy concerns | Directly links youth perspectives to privacy‑policy debates [3] |
| **High‑Level Session 5 – Protecting Children’s Rights** | Youth‑focused recommendations | Calls for solutions that give children the means to enjoy digital rights [4] |
| **Digitalise Youth Project (Kofi Annan Foundation)** | Youth activists & CSOs | Build capacity, address disinformation, promote digital rights regionally [5] |

---

### Key Take‑aways for Policymakers & Stakeholders

1. **Institutionalise Youth Participation** – Embed youth representatives in data‑governance bodies, AI design committees, and human‑rights forums.  
2. **Invest in Capacity‑Building** – Provide training, mentorship, and resources (as done by the Digitalise Youth Project) so youth can engage confidently and sustainably.  
3. **Center Child‑Specific Safeguards** – Ensure that any digital‑policy or AI system explicitly addresses the unique vulnerabilities of children and adolescents.  
4. **Leverage Youth Networks for Counter‑Disinformation** – Youth‑led civil‑society groups are effective frontline actors against misinformation, especially in fragile contexts.  

By listening to, empowering, and institutionalising youth voices, the digital ecosystem becomes more inclusive, rights‑respecting, and resilient.  

**Sources**: [1], [2], [3], [4], [5], [7], [8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Youth voices are increasingly recognised as essential drivers of fair and effective digital‑rights policies.  Across the sessions and initiatives cited, several key points emerge:

1. **Youth participation improves equity and effectiveness** – In WS #32 “Harnessing Youth Voices to Transform the Data Economy,” participants stress that involving young people in digital governance leads to more equitable and workable policies, because they bring lived experience of the vulnerabilities that the digital age creates for them [1][7].

2. **Youth bring unique perspectives on privacy and AI** – Representatives such as **Joon Baek** of *Youth for Privacy* highlighted at WS #376 “Elevating Children’s Voices in AI Design” that youth‑led NGOs can spotlight privacy concerns and push for AI systems that respect children’s rights [3].

3. **Children’s rights are a specific focus** – The High‑Level Session 5 on “Protecting Children’s Rights in the Digital World” called for concrete solutions that give children and youth the tools and opportunities to shape the digital environment they inhabit [4].

4. **Capacity‑building and advocacy are linked** – The Kofi Annan Foundation’s *Digitalise Youth* project, part of its Digital Democracy Initiative, demonstrates how building youth capacity (training, networking) translates into stronger advocacy for digital rights at local, regional, and international levels, especially in contexts where civic space is shrinking and disinformation is rampant [5].

5. **Youth are both vulnerable and powerful agents of change** – The workshops repeatedly note that while young people face “unique vulnerabilities in the digital age” (e.g., data exploitation, privacy breaches), they also have the potential to act as catalysts for positive policy reform when their voices are amplified [1][7][8].

### What this means for digital‑rights work
- **Include youth in policy‑making bodies** (e.g., advisory councils, data‑governance forums) to ensure policies reflect their lived realities.  
- **Support youth‑led NGOs** such as Youth for Privacy that specialize in digital‑privacy advocacy.  
- **Invest in capacity‑building programs** like Digitalise Youth to equip young activists with the skills needed to counter disinformation and lobby for rights.  
- **Design AI and data systems with children’s input**, as advocated in WS #376, to embed child‑rights safeguards from the outset.  

In short, amplifying youth voices not only addresses their specific digital vulnerabilities but also enriches the overall digital‑rights agenda with fresh, rights‑focused perspectives. [1][3][4][5][7][8]

</details>

> **Judge:** Both answers are accurate and cover the main social and economic impacts, but A lists a broader set of points and cites sources more comprehensively, giving it a slight edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Social impacts**

* **Public‑service transformation** – Digital technologies have reshaped how individuals and communities obtain public services and interact with one another, creating new opportunities but also raising concerns about human‑rights, education, health, employment and overall welfare [1].  
* **Conflict‑related disruption** – Armed conflict generates massive social fallout, breaking community cohesion and damaging livelihoods [2].  
* **Unequal exposure to risk** – In the mobility sector, road‑traffic accidents disproportionately affect productive population groups, amplifying social inequities [6].  
* **Platform‑driven change** – The rapid expansion of digital platforms alters social relations, influencing everything from civic participation to the way people consume information [7].  
* **Inequality‑induced political strain** – Growing socioeconomic inequality can produce profound political consequences that erode social trust and destabilise societies [8].

**Economic impacts**

* **Productivity and employment shifts** – While digitalisation can boost efficiency, it also creates employment challenges and can jeopardise welfare for certain groups [1].  
* **Broad‑spectrum economic consequences** – Any technical innovation can trigger a range of economic outcomes, from new market opportunities to disruption of existing business models; the full set of effects is not exhaustive [3][4].  
* **Conflict‑driven economic loss** – The human and social costs of conflict translate into massive economic damage for affected nations [2].  
* **Platform economics** – Digital platforms generate new revenue streams and market structures, but they also reshape competition, labor markets and tax bases [7].  
* **Growth‑impairing inequality** – Deepening inequality can undermine economic growth by creating governance challenges (legislative deadlock, paralysis) that hinder effective policy implementation [8].

**Overall picture**

The sources converge on the view that digital and technological change, as well as conflict, produce intertwined social and economic effects. Positive transformations in service delivery and productivity are counterbalanced by risks to human rights, health, employment, and social equity. Moreover, rising inequality can feed back into political instability, further threatening economic performance. [1][2][3][5][6][7][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Social impacts mentioned in the sources**

* **Changes to everyday life and public services** – Digital technologies have reshaped how people interact, consume information and spend their time, affecting the delivery of public services and community life [1].  
* **Human‑rights and welfare concerns** – The same transformations raise issues for human rights, education, health, employment and the overall welfare of individuals and communities [1].  
* **Conflict‑related social disruption** – Armed conflict produces massive human and social consequences for affected populations [2].  
* **Road‑safety inequality** – In the mobility sector, road accidents hit productive population groups disproportionately, creating a notable social burden [6].  
* **Governance and political stability** – Growing inequality can generate profound political consequences, such as legislative dead‑locks or even government paralysis, which in turn affect social cohesion [8].  
* **Broad digital‑platform effects** – The expansion of digital platforms generates wide‑ranging social (and political) ramifications, from changes in communication patterns to shifts in civic engagement [7].

**Economic impacts mentioned in the sources**

* **Employment and labour markets** – Digital transformation influences employment opportunities and the nature of work, with both positive and negative effects on labour markets [1].  
* **Economic consequences of conflict** – The social and economic costs of conflict are described as “massive,” implying large losses in productivity, infrastructure and growth [2].  
* **Technical impacts generate diverse economic outcomes** – Any category of technical change can produce a variety of economic consequences; the list of possible effects is not exhaustive [3][4].  
* **Inequality undermining growth** – Political fallout from inequality can directly weaken economic growth by making governance harder, leading to legislative impasses or paralysis [8].  
* **Digital platforms as economic drivers** – The rising use of digital platforms creates new economic dynamics, reshaping markets, business models and value creation [7].  
* **Mobility sector costs** – Road accidents impose economic costs, especially on productive segments of the population, affecting overall economic efficiency [6].

**Overall picture**

Across the cited documents, the social and economic impacts of digital and technological change are portrayed as **deeply intertwined**. They affect how people live, learn, stay healthy and interact, while simultaneously reshaping labour markets, growth prospects, and the capacity of governments to function effectively. The sources stress that these impacts are **multifaceted and not exhaustively listed**, indicating that each technical development can generate a broad spectrum of social and economic outcomes [3][4].

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a multistakeholder platform created to foster open, inclusive dialogue on public‑policy issues that affect the Internet.  It was established under Paragraph 72 of the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS) and serves as a venue where governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia can discuss the key elements of Internet governance — but it does not make binding decisions; rather, it seeks to identify solutions through discussion [1][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a multistakeholder platform created under Paragraph 72 of the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS).  It provides an open, inclusive space for dialogue on public‑policy issues that affect the key elements of Internet governance.  The forum brings together governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia to discuss topics such as access, security, diversity and openness, to help find solutions to problems arising from the use and misuse of the Internet—especially for users in developing countries.  Importantly, the IGF is designed for **discussion and consensus‑building, not for formal decision‑making**【1†L1-L3】【3†L1-L5】【8†L1-L3】.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**“Test diplomacy” can be understood in two related ways that emerge from the supplied excerpts.**

1. **Diplomacy that is built around a “test” – e.g., nuclear‑test‑ban negotiations**  
   The classic example is the series of nuclear‑test‑ban treaties that were negotiated bilaterally (Soviet‑American) and later multilateral‑ly (USA, USSR, UK).  These treaties were expressly designed to **ban the testing of nuclear weapons** in the atmosphere, underwater and in outer space, and the negotiations themselves were a form of diplomatic testing of how the great powers could reach agreement on a highly sensitive security issue【3†L1-L4】.  The same pattern is noted in two other passages that describe the nuclear test‑ban as a “good example” of diplomacy in action【1†L1-L3】【2†L1-L3】.

2. **Diplomacy that is itself “tested” or treated as a beta‑version**  
   A more conceptual use of the term appears in the discussion of modern diplomatic practice.  Here, diplomacy is likened to software that is **“functional but still being tested, adapted, and iterated in real time.”**  The “beta” metaphor emphasizes that diplomatic rules, institutions and norms are incomplete and subject to continual experimentation, especially as new technologies (e.g., algorithms) are deployed【4†L1-L4】.  This framing treats diplomatic processes as experimental tests rather than fixed, final products.

**Putting it together:**  
“Test diplomacy” therefore refers either to diplomatic initiatives that **address the testing of weapons** (such as nuclear‑test‑ban treaties) or to a broader view of diplomacy as an **ongoing experimental practice**—a “beta” system that is constantly being tried out, refined, and evaluated in real‑time contexts. Both senses are reflected in the provided material.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**How “testing” appears in diplomatic practice – what the sources say**

| Aspect of “testing” | What the source describes | Diplomatic relevance |
|--------------------|---------------------------|----------------------|

| **Disarmament diplomacy** | *Disarmament diplomacy* points out that the 1963 Nuclear Test Ban Treaty was an agreement among the USA, the Soviet Union and the UK to ban testing “in the atmosphere, underwater, and in outer space.” | A concrete example of **testing** a new normative regime (a ban on nuclear tests) through multilateral negotiation. |
| **Diplomacy as a “beta” system** | *Diplomacy in beta: From Geneva principles to Abu Dhabi deliberations in the age of algorithms* describes today’s diplomacy as “functional but still being tested, adapted, and iterated in real time,” with incomplete rules and lagging institutions. | Highlights that modern diplomatic processes are **continually tested** against fast‑moving technological and policy changes (e.g., AI, internet governance). |
| **Security diplomacy – low‑pressure testing** | *Security diplomacy* notes that such forums “allow for testing new ideas and building personal relationships in a low‑pressure environment.” | Provides a space where diplomatic actors can **experiment** with concepts before scaling them to higher‑stakes negotiations. |
| **Psychological testing of diplomats** | *Diplomatic Training around the World* lists a series of psychological instruments (Rorschach, TAT, sentence completion, EPPS, interviews) that “should be given again when the diplomat begins to make decisions, lead working teams, and hold executive positions.” | Uses **formal tests** to assess and develop the personal capacities of diplomats, ensuring they are fit for decision‑making roles. |
| **Testing platforms** | *Diplomacy.edu website* simply states “It was probably used for testing purposes.” | Implies that digital tools for diplomatic training or outreach may be **piloted** before full deployment. |

### Key take‑aways

1. **Test‑ban negotiations** are a historic example of diplomacy creating a concrete prohibition on a specific activity (nuclear testing) – a literal “test” of the international community’s ability to enforce non‑proliferation [1][2][3].
2. Modern diplomatic work is framed as a **beta‑stage system** that is continuously tested and refined in response to new technologies and policy challenges [4].
3. **Security‑diplomacy settings** serve as experimental labs where new ideas can be tried out without the pressure of high‑stakes negotiations [6].
4. **Diplomatic training** incorporates formal **psychological testing** to evaluate and improve the competencies of diplomats as they assume greater responsibilities [7].
5. Even the **digital infrastructure** that supports diplomatic learning (e.g., the Diplomacy.edu site) may be used in a testing capacity before wider rollout [8].

Together, these snippets illustrate that “testing” is woven into diplomacy at several levels: from the content of treaties (test bans) to the processes (beta‑style iteration), the environments for idea‑testing (security diplomacy), and the personal assessment of diplomats themselves.

**Guidelines for Sharing Research and Experiences (based on the provided context)**

| 1. **Leverage practical experience** | When deciding what to share, draw on hands‑on work you have done rather than only on written results. | *“Practical experience helps develop judgment on sharing research”* – [1] & [6] |
| 2. **Balance openness with risk** | Weigh the benefit of feedback against the danger of being scooped or exposing security‑sensitive details. | *“Researchers deciding whether to present new results at conferences, balancing the risk of being scooped against the benefits of feedback”* – [6] |
| 3. **Use multiple formats** | Complement formal documents with narratives, case studies, or live demonstrations of what you actually did. | *“How we show the fact or knowledge, experience, sharing those things is quite important, not only by document, by real experience.”* – [2] |
| 4. **Share across layers and regions** | Distribute information not just within your own team or sector but also between different stakeholder layers (e.g., technical, policy, management) and geographic regions. | *“Information sharing should not be only within certain layers but also between those layers to build understanding.”* – [8] |
| 5. **Participate in open forums and networking sessions** | Present your findings at venues such as IGF open forums, CSIRT practitioner meetings, or high‑level sessions where peers expect experience exchange. | *“So if we're going to share information, we should be able to share our experiences.”* – [7] <br>*“Different regions have different experiences that can be shared for mutual benefit.”* – [8] |
| 6. **Encourage two‑way exchange** | Invite others to comment, ask questions, and contribute their own observations; treat sharing as a dialogue, not a one‑way broadcast. | *“So sharing information is one thing… we will exchange our comments and try to understand what was going to be discussed.”* – [4] |
| 7. **Highlight the collaborative value** | Emphasize how data and experience sharing fuels collaboration, accelerates knowledge exchange, and builds trust among stakeholders. | *“Data sharing promotes collaboration and knowledge exchange.”* – [3] |
| 8. **Document lessons learned** | After each sharing event, capture what worked, what didn’t, and any follow‑up actions so future participants can benefit. | Implicit in the repeated call to “share experiences” and “exchange comments” across sessions – [2], [4], [8] |

**Practical Tips for Implementation**

- **Prepare a concise “experience brief”** that pairs key data points with a short narrative of the real‑world context (e.g., a CSIRT incident response timeline).  
- **Use open‑access platforms** (conference proceedings, IGF session recordings, community repositories) to make the material discoverable.  
- **Add a risk‑assessment note** that flags any sensitive elements and explains why they are being disclosed or omitted.  
- **Invite feedback loops** (e‑mail, follow‑up webinars, Q&A panels) to turn a static presentation into an ongoing knowledge‑exchange process.  

By following these steps—grounded in the experiences and recommendations highlighted across the Davos, IGF, and CSIRT sessions—you can share research and practical experiences responsibly, effectively, and with maximal benefit for the broader community.

**Key take‑aways from the cited sessions on how to share research and experiences**

| **Regional experiences are valuable** – Different locales have distinct practices that can benefit others when shared across “layers” of the community【8†L1-L4】. | • Create regional “experience briefs” (1‑2 page PDFs) and circulate them through global platforms (IGF, DPI sessions, CSIRT networks). <br>• Encourage cross‑layer dialogue: senior policy makers, technical teams, and end‑users should all be invited to the same discussion. |
| **Structured networking sessions work** – Dedicated networking slots (e.g., IGF 2023 Session #44, DPI High‑Level Session) allow participants to surface interesting observations and disseminate them quickly【4†L1-L3】【5†L1-L2】. | • When planning a conference, allocate a “rapid‑share” block (5‑minute lightning talks) for attendees to broadcast a recent finding or incident. <br>• Capture the output in a shared document that all participants can edit post‑event. |
| **Balancing openness with security** – Young scientists need guidance on research ethics and security when deciding what to share【6†L1-L4】. | • Offer a short ethics checklist before submission (e.g., “Is the data sensitive? Could disclosure cause harm?”). <br>• Provide mentorship or peer‑review of the sharing plan, especially for high‑risk topics. |

---

### Practical checklist for sharing research **and** experiences

1. **Define the audience** – Are you speaking to peers, policy makers, or the public? Tailor the depth of technical detail and the narrative style accordingly.  
2. **Combine artefacts** – Attach a peer‑reviewed paper **and** a concise experience note (max 300 words) that explains the “how” and “why”.  
3. **Choose the right venue** – Use formal channels for the paper (journals, repositories) and informal or hybrid channels for experience (IGF open forums, CSIRT networking sessions, webinars).  
4. **Apply a risk‑benefit filter** – Ask: *What is the benefit of sharing now?* vs. *What is the risk of being scooped or causing security concerns?* (as highlighted in the Davos discussion)【1†L1-L3】【6†L1-L4】.  
5. **Document metadata and context** – Include provenance, methodology, and any regional nuances that could affect interpretation (see regional sharing insight)【8†L1-L4】.  
6. **Facilitate two‑way exchange** – Invite questions, encourage others to share their own experiences, and record the dialogue for future reference.  
7. **Maintain ethical standards** – Follow the “research ethics and security” guidance for young scientists, ensuring that any shared data comply with privacy, export‑control, and intellectual‑property rules【6†L1-L4】.  

---

### Quick “how‑to” example

| Step | Action | Tool / Platform |
|------|--------|-----------------|
| 1 | Write a short “experience brief” (≤ 1 page) summarizing a recent experiment, including what you learned and any unexpected outcomes. | Google Docs / Overleaf |
| 2 | Upload the associated dataset to a trusted repository (e.g., Zenodo) with a clear CC‑BY license. | Zenodo, Figshare |
| 3 | Submit the brief and dataset link to an IGF open‑forum session or a CSIRT practitioner meetup. | IGF conference portal, Slack/Discord community |
| 4 | During the session, give a 5‑minute oral summary, field questions, and note feedback. | Zoom/Teams |
| 5 | Post‑session, add the Q&A and any additional insights to a shared knowledge base (e.g., a GitHub wiki). | GitHub, Confluence |
| 6 | Run the ethics checklist before publishing to ensure no sensitive information is exposed. | Internal checklist template |

By **pairing formal research outputs with lived‑experience narratives**, and by using the multi‑layered sharing mechanisms highlighted across the Davos, IGF, and CSIRT sessions, you can maximise the impact of your work while respecting security and ethical considerations.

</details>

### Q93: what is ECMAScript ?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |

| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers the definition, spec details, implementation aspects and provides a concise summary, using more citations, giving it higher completeness and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and JScript. It is the language defined by Ecma International’s ECMA‑262 specification and is an essential part of the Web browser application platform, while also being used in many other environments—including embedded systems, micro‑controllers and single‑board computers【1†L1-L4】【2†L1-L4】.  

The language specification defines its syntax, semantics, execution environment and the built‑in libraries that programs can use【5†L1-L4】. An ECMAScript implementation therefore consists of the language itself together with required (and optional) built‑in libraries【6†L1-L3】.  

In short, ECMAScript is the standardized programming language that powers JavaScript on the web and increasingly powers a wide range of software beyond the browser.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript.  It is the language defined by Ecma International’s ECMA‑262 specification and is a core part of the Web browser application platform, while also being used in many other environments such as embedded systems, micro‑controllers and single‑board computers.  The language specification describes its syntax, semantics, execution environment and built‑in libraries, and the specifications are maintained by Ecma International’s TC39 committee [1][4][5].

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer A covers a broader range of click scenarios, offers concrete tips, and cites sources more extensively, making it more complete and specific.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Your Clicks, Your Rights – How the Simple Act of “Click‑ing” Connects to Your Online Human‑Rights**

| What you do when you click | Which right(s) are involved (as set out in the provided documents) | How the click can affect the exercise of that right |
|----------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Click “I Agree” to a Terms‑of‑Service (ToS) contract** | • Right to peacefully assemble and associate online, and to choose the tools (websites, apps, services) you use [1]  <br>• Right to control personal data and to know it will not be used against you [6] | By accepting a ToS you give the service provider permission to set the rules for how you may assemble, associate, or share data on that platform. If the ToS contains clauses that limit data‑use transparency or allow profiling, the right to control personal data may be curtailed. |
| **Click “Accept” on a privacy‑policy or data‑sale opt‑out** | • Right to control personal data and to opt‑out of its sale [6] | The click activates a legal mechanism that either grants the provider permission to process your data (if you accept) or blocks that processing (if you opt‑out). Your click therefore directly determines whether the right to data‑control is respected. |
| **Click “Share” or “Post” content** | • Right to receive and impart information, create, reuse and distribute content [5]  <br>• Right to express yourself freely and be treated with respect [3] | The platform’s ToS may contain editorial policies that restrict hate‑based speech. By clicking “share,” you are exercising the right to impart information, but the provider may intervene if the content violates those policies. |
| **Click “No” or “Block” when a request makes you uncomfortable** | • Right to say NO if someone makes you feel uncomfortable [3]  <br>• Right to protect children from collection of personal data [6] | The act of clicking “decline,” “block,” or “report” is the practical expression of the right to refuse unwanted interaction or data collection. The platform must honor that click for the right to be meaningful. |
| **Click a search‑result or advertisement** | • Right to understand how search‑engine results are manipulated [6] | Clicking on a result is a data point that can be used to shape future rankings. The Digital Bill of Rights demands transparency about that manipulation, so your click should be accompanied by the right to know how it influences the ecosystem. |

### Why “Clicks” Matter for Your Rights  

1. **Clicks are the modern “contractual handshake.”**  
   The Electronic Frontier Foundation notes that “the clicks that bind” are the primary way users consent to Terms of Service [2]. Each click creates a legal relationship that can either **protect** or **limit** the rights listed in the human‑rights‑for‑Internet‑users guides (peaceful assembly, data control, free expression, etc.) [1],[5].

2. **Clicks translate abstract rights into concrete platform behavior.**  
   - When you click to **agree** to a ToS, you implicitly accept the platform’s rules on how it may treat your speech, data, and association.  
   - When you click to **opt‑out** of data sale, you activate the right to control personal data [6].  
   - When you click **“No”** to an uncomfortable request, you exercise the child‑online‑protection right to refuse [3].

3. **Clicks can be a point of accountability.**  
   Because the act of clicking is recorded, it can be used as evidence that a user **exercised** a right (e.g., opting out) or that a provider **failed** to respect that right (e.g., continuing to sell data after an opt‑out). This aligns with the broader principle that “everyone also has the right to associate freely through and on the Internet” [8].

### Practical Tips to Make Your Clicks Work for Your Rights  

| Situation | Click‑action | How it safeguards the right |
|-----------|--------------|------------------------------|
| **Signing up for a new service** | Read the ToS and privacy policy; click **“I do not agree”** if the data‑use clauses are too broad. | Preserves your right to control personal data [6]. |
| **Posting content** | Review community guidelines; click **“Post”** only if your content complies. | Ensures your right to free expression is respected while avoiding removal under editorial policies [5]. |
| **Receiving a request for personal info** | Click **“Decline”** or **“Report”** if the request feels invasive. | Enforces the right to say NO and protects identity [3]. |
| **Using a search engine** | Click **“Learn more about how results are ranked”** (if offered). | Activates the right to understand manipulation of search results [6]. |
| **Child‑focused app** | Click the **“Parental consent”** or **“Opt‑out of data collection”** button. | Upholds the right to protect children from data harvesting [6]. |

### Bottom Line  

Every time you click—whether to accept terms, share a post, block a user, or opt‑out of data collection—you are **exercising** one of the human‑rights‑based freedoms recognized for Internet users. Understanding which right each click touches lets you **use your clicks as tools** for safeguarding privacy, free expression, association, and safety online.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Your clicks are not just a technical step – they are a legal act that can shape how your digital rights are respected or limited.**  

| What you click on | What it can affect | The right that still belongs to you (according to the sources) |
|--------------------|-------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
| **“I Agree” to a Terms‑of‑Service (ToS) or Privacy Policy** | The service may set rules about what you can post, how your data is used, or when your account can be suspended. | • **Right to peacefully assemble and associate online** – you should be free to choose the tools (websites, apps, services) you use for gathering with others [1].  <br>• **Right to receive and impart information** – you may seek, create, reuse and distribute content without interference [5].  <br>• **Right to control personal data** – you have the right to know your data won’t be used against you and to opt‑out of its sale [6]. |
| **“I Accept” cookies or tracking preferences** | Determines whether your browsing behaviour is recorded and possibly sold to third parties. | • **Right to protect your identity** and to be treated with respect while online [3].  <br>• **Right to be informed** about how search engines manipulate results [6]. |
| **“I consent” to age‑verification or parental‑control settings** | May limit the content you can see or the services you can use. | • **Right to access information appropriate to your age** and to say “NO” if something makes you uncomfortable [3]. |
| **“I agree” to a platform’s community‑guidelines** | Gives the platform authority to remove or flag content that it deems hateful or biased. | • **Right to free expression** while respecting others, and the expectation that providers will not silence speech merely because it is unpopular [5]. |

### Why the click matters
The Electronic Frontier Foundation notes that “clicks” are the primary way users **agree** to the contractual terms that govern their online experience [2]. Those contracts can limit or expand the exercise of the rights listed above.  

### How to protect your rights when you click
1. **Read the ToS/Privacy Policy** – even a brief skim can reveal whether the service reserves the right to delete content, share data, or restrict access.  
2. **Check for digital‑bill‑of‑rights provisions** – some jurisdictions (e.g., Florida) have codified rights such as data‑control and protection for children [6].  
3. **Exercise the right to say “NO.”** If a request or a clause makes you uncomfortable, you can refuse or look for an alternative service [3].  
4. **Know the broader human‑rights framework** – the Charter of Human Rights for the Internet affirms that everyone may associate freely online for social, political, cultural, or other purposes [8].  

### Bottom line
Every “I agree” click is a legal acknowledgement that can shape how your **freedom of assembly, expression, information, and data‑control** are respected. By staying aware of the rights you retain—right to assemble, to receive/impart information, to protect your identity, and to control your personal data—you can make more informed choices about the platforms you use.

</details>

### Q97: How did 9/11 reforms influence digital diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.17s | 4.81s (+0.10s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 3.5/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 6 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** B addresses the question more fully, adds nuanced points about broader policy areas, and references a wider range of sources.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The terrorist attacks of 9/11 triggered a sweeping re‑organisation of U.S. intelligence and foreign‑policy structures that directly shaped the way the United States now conducts “digital diplomacy.”

* **Breaking down silos** – In the wake of 9/11 the U.S. intelligence community was over‑hauled to eliminate the fragmentation and policy‑silo problems that had hampered threat detection.  That same logic was applied to the diplomatic corps: the State Department was instructed to “plug the gaps in information sharing between various departments” so that diplomats could work with a fuller, more timely picture of global events [5].

* **From “need‑to‑know” to “need‑to‑share”** – The post‑9/11 reforms shifted the culture of U.S. foreign‑policy institutions from restricting information to a “need‑to‑know” mindset toward a “need‑to‑share” approach.  The State Department therefore became an early adopter of social‑media platforms and other online tools, deliberately using them to disseminate information quickly and to engage wider audiences [8].

* **A holistic digital‑diplomacy agenda** – Because the reforms emphasized integrated, cross‑agency collaboration, the United States now pursues a “holistic digital diplomacy” strategy that treats the internet, social media, and other ICTs as core instruments for meetings, negotiations, information‑sharing, and public‑diplomacy initiatives [1][6].

* **Security remains a driver, but the scope is broader** – While 9/11 placed security and counter‑terrorism at the top of the multilateral agenda, the reforms did not push other issues (poverty, disease, democracy) out of the diplomatic mix; instead, the new digital tools allow those topics to be addressed alongside security concerns [4].

**In short, the 9/11‑driven reforms forced the United States to dismantle information silos, adopt a culture of open sharing, and embed internet‑based technologies into every stage of diplomatic work—giving rise to the modern, holistic practice of digital diplomacy.**

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The reforms that followed the September‑11 attacks reshaped the way the United States conducts diplomacy by turning the old “need‑to‑know” culture into a “need‑to‑share” one.  After 9/11 U.S. intelligence agencies were reorganised to eliminate the fragmentation and policy silos that had hampered threat‑prevention — a change that was later extended to the diplomatic corps [1].  The same drive to plug information‑sharing gaps was applied to the State Department, where diplomats were given broader, real‑time access to global data so they could “better do their jobs” [5].  With that new openness, the State Department deliberately adopted internet‑based tools and social‑media platforms, turning them into core diplomatic instruments rather than peripheral curiosities [8].  

In practice this produced what scholars now call **holistic digital diplomacy**: a diplomatic approach that integrates security‑driven information‑sharing reforms with the wider use of ICTs, social media, and online public‑diplomacy initiatives.  The post‑9/11 shift therefore:

*  broke down inter‑agency silos and created a culture of continuous information exchange;  
*  gave diplomats direct, digital access to the same intelligence streams that had previously been compartmentalised;  
*  encouraged the State Department to embrace the internet and social media as official channels for negotiation, reporting, and public outreach; and  
*  positioned digital tools as a permanent, security‑aware layer of U.S. diplomatic practice [1][5][8].

Thus, the 9/11 reforms did not just tighten security—they also catalysed the United States’ move toward a more integrated, technology‑driven form of diplomacy.

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer B covers an extra function (supporting Geneva‑based institutions) and lists the points more explicitly, with slightly richer detail and broader citation usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The Geneva Internet Platform (GIP) is a Swiss‑run initiative that is operated by the DiploFoundation. Its purpose is to bring together digital‑sector actors in a neutral, inclusive space where they can discuss and shape digital‑policy issues. The platform works to 

* foster digital governance and monitor digital policies,  
* strengthen the participation of small and developing countries in Geneva‑based digital policy processes,  
* support the work of Geneva‑based Internet‑governance and ICT institutions,  
* facilitate evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research for digital policy,  
* bridge the various policy “silos,” and  
* provide tools and methods for both in‑situ and online engagement that can be used by other policy spaces worldwide.  

These functions are described in the platform’s own description [1][2][5].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The Geneva Internet Platform (GIP) is a Swiss‑based initiative run by the DiploFoundation. It was created to bring together digital actors in a neutral, inclusive space where they can debate and shape digital policy. Its core functions are to engage stakeholders, foster Internet‑governance and digital‑governance processes, monitor emerging digital policies, and support evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research. GIP also works to strengthen the participation of small and developing countries in Geneva‑based digital policy fora, bridge the various policy “silos,” and provide tools and methods for both in‑situ and online engagement that can be used by other policy spaces worldwide [1][5].

</details>

### Q99: what was the norvegian policy during covid?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 6.89s | 5.38s (+0.06s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are accurate, but B adds the open‑source digital‑public‑goods aspect and cites an extra source, making it slightly more complete, specific, and well‑referenced.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Norwegian COVID‑19 policy was a multi‑layered mix of public‑health, economic‑relief and digital‑transformation measures, with a strong emphasis on privacy and open‑source solutions.**  

| Policy area | What Norway did during the pandemic | Source |
|-------------|--------------------------------------|--------|
| **Public‑health & social distancing** | Rapidly moved the population to a “new normal” of home‑working, home‑schooling and minimal physical contact once the virus hit. | 【2】 |
| **Cross‑border trade & food supply** | Recognised that infection‑control measures disrupted cross‑border shopping and the production/sale of food and drink, prompting sector‑specific monitoring. | 【3】 |
| **Economic safety net** | The Parliament quickly drafted and enacted statutes and regulations that **strengthened the economic safety net** for households and businesses affected by the crisis. | 【5】 |
| **Digital acceleration** | The pandemic was used as a catalyst for a broad digitalisation push: Norway was already well‑positioned, and the crisis accelerated digital transformation across the public sector, aquaculture, maritime, agriculture, tourism and other industries. | 【6】 |
| **Privacy‑first contact‑tracing** | Norway **stopped its COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app** after the data‑protection authority warned that processing personal data would be temporarily banned; all collected data were deleted. | 【4】 |
| **Open‑source, open‑public‑goods approach** | The government’s longer‑term digital strategy (discussed in the Open Forum) stresses **digital public goods that are open, non‑commercial and avoid vendor lock‑in**, reinforcing democratic principles even during the emergency. | 【8】 |
| **Future‑oriented recovery** | A 2021 official report (“Norway towards 2025”) recommended that pandemic‑related restrictions be lifted when prudent and outlined goals for competitiveness, green growth, a robust labour market and sustainable welfare. | 【7】 |

### In short
During COVID‑19 Norway’s policy combined:

1. **Strict public‑health measures** (home‑working, schooling, limited contact).  
2. **Rapid, legislated economic support** to protect households and firms.  
3. **A fast‑track digitalisation agenda** that leveraged the crisis to modernise public services and key industries.  
4. **A privacy‑protective stance** that led to the shutdown of the national tracing app.  
5. **Commitment to open, non‑proprietary digital solutions** to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic values.  

These elements together formed Norway’s overall pandemic response.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

During the COVID‑19 pandemic Norway pursued a **multifaceted policy that combined strict public‑health measures, rapid economic relief, and an accelerated push for digitalisation – while keeping data‑privacy safeguards at the forefront**.

| Policy area | What Norway did | Source |
|-------------|----------------|--------|
| **Public‑health & social distancing** | The “new normal” was defined by home‑working, home‑schooling and minimal physical contact as soon as the virus hit the country. | [2] |
| **Economic safety net** | The Parliament quickly turned pandemic‑related decisions into statutes and regulations that **strengthened the economic safety net** for households and businesses. | [5] |
| **Digital transformation** | The crisis was used as a catalyst for digitalisation: the public sector was described as “pioneering”, and a broad digital‑transformation programme was rolled out across aquaculture, maritime, agriculture, tourism and other sectors. | [6] |
| **Cross‑border & food‑supply impacts** | Measures limited cross‑border shopping and affected production and sales of food and drink, prompting policy attention to those supply‑chain disruptions. | [3] |
| **Data‑privacy & contact‑tracing** | Norway **stopped its COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app** and deleted all data after the national data‑protection authority warned that processing personal information would be temporarily banned. This reflects a policy choice to prioritize privacy over continued digital tracking. | [4] |
| **Future outlook** | The government signalled that restrictive measures should be lifted “as soon as it is prudent”, with an official report (March 2021) outlining goals for competitiveness, green growth, a well‑functioning labour market and long‑term economic sustainability. | [7] |

**In short:** Norway’s COVID‑19 policy was characterised by:

1. **Strict containment** (home‑working/schooling, limited contact).  
2. **Rapid economic support** through new statutes and regulations.  
3. **Accelerated digitalisation** across public services and key industries.  
4. **Strong data‑privacy protection**, exemplified by the shutdown of the contact‑tracing app.  
5. **A forward‑looking plan** to unwind restrictions while ensuring competitiveness and sustainability.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Below is a concise list of the concrete programmes and collaborative mechanisms that the supplied sources identify as actively **advancing digital rights**:

| Initiative | Main Activities that Promote Digital Rights | Reference |

| **Advancing Digital Democracy (ADD) – USAID** | • Builds an open, secure and inclusive digital ecosystem through multi‑stakeholder partnerships (governments, private sector, civil society). <br>• Strengthens legal and regulatory frameworks for data and digital technologies. <br>• Provides support to software engineers, tech firms and researchers to embed human‑rights and democratic values across the tech lifecycle. | [2] |
| **IRPC Human Rights Law & the Global Digital Compact (IGF 2023)** | • Engages organisations and volunteers to raise awareness of digital rights. <br>• Collaborates with a range of partners to promote a rights‑based approach to digital policy and practice. | [3] |
| **Digitalise Youth Project – Kofi Annan Foundation (Digital Democracy Initiative)** | • Enhances digital skills and civic‑tech solutions for youth. <br>• Conducts advocacy to promote digital rights at local, regional and international levels. <br>• Targets shrinking civic space and disinformation in the Sahel and neighbouring regions by empowering youth activists and CSOs. | [6] |
| **Global Digital Rights Coalition (GDRC) coordination efforts** | • Provides a platform for coordinated action among digital‑rights organisations, facilitating joint advocacy, policy monitoring and capacity‑building. | [8] |
| **Rights and Risk Forums** | • Convene stakeholders to discuss regulatory implementation, identify rights‑related risks and share best practices for safeguarding digital rights. | [8] |
| **EU diplomatic initiatives on digital rights** | • Diplomatic outreach and policy dialogue aimed at embedding digital‑rights standards in bilateral and multilateral agreements, and supporting implementation of rights‑respecting regulations. | [8] |
| **Calls for legislation & digital public‑infrastructure implementation** (broader policy push) | • Urges states to enact laws that integrate digital rights and to ensure that digital public infrastructure is built and operated in line with human‑rights norms. | [7] |

**Key take‑aways**

1. **Multi‑stakeholder partnerships** (ADD, GDRC, Rights & Risk Forums, EU diplomatic work) are repeatedly highlighted as the engine for translating digital‑rights principles into concrete policy and technical outcomes.  
2. **Capacity‑building and youth empowerment** (Digitalise Youth, ADD’s support for engineers) are seen as essential for creating a rights‑aware digital ecosystem.  
3. **Legal and regulatory reform** (ADD, the broader legislative push in [7]) is central to institutionalising digital rights and ensuring they are protected in the design and operation of digital infrastructure.  

These initiatives together illustrate a growing ecosystem of programmes—spanning government, multilateral, civil‑society and private‑sector actors—that are actively working to embed and protect digital rights worldwide.
